19 December 1994

— ‘Likely’ To Be Postponed Until Feb

LD1612174494 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1714
GMT 16 Dec 94

[By BELINFORM correspondent Svetlana Kalinkina for
TASS]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Minsk December 16—The CIS
summit of heads of state scheduled for December 23,
1994 in Alma-Ata [Almaty] is likely to be postponed for
early February 1995, CIS executive secretary Ivan
Korotchenya told at a meeting with the Ukrainian dele-
gation today.

The BELINFORM news agency was told by sources that
the delay was not officially announced because Kaza-
khstan, the host state, has not formulated its response so
far.

CIS leaders have signed a letter to Russian President
Boris Yeltsin, saying that the summit was put off because
agenda items were not urgent and required more thor-
ough preparation.

It is expected that Kazakhstan will make an official
statement on the matter late on Friday. Meanwhile,
February 10 is cited as a tentative new date.

Railroad Chiefs Agree To Resume Cooperation

MM 1612144394 Moscow ROSSIYSKIYE VESTI in
Russian 16 Dec 94 p 2

[Report by Tatyana Kolysko under the “Transport”
rubric: “CIS Railroads: Is There a Way Cut of the
Siding?”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] The division of the railroads
after the disintegration of the Union had a hypothetical
au aboui +t Decause they are the property of all the states
in comm pn use.

But while some peopie got the bagel, others got the hole
in the middle: Many republics lost their repair base or
were left without facilities for the production of ties and
rails. In the Transcaucasus, for example, thousands of
freightcars are standing idle—the railroads are blocked.
Russia inherited 70 percent of the freightcar mainte-
nance load. Rail shipments are being maintained now
only by using old cars and reducing commodity circula-
tion.

All these and many other problems were discussed in
Moscow at the 12th session of the CIS states’ rail
transport council, with the participation of the Baltic
states.

In conditions when economic cooperation between the
former republics is being revived, albeit slowly and
arduously, disaster can strike from where you least
expect it—delay in the solution of railroad problems
could paralyze the work of transport.

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

It seems that everyone has understood that. The govern-
ments of all the states are beginning to face up to
transport problems.

After some arguments and mutual recriminations the
leaders of the CIS railroads found a way out of the siding
they were in, and eventually it was decided that all
reciprocal debts would be frozen and new settlements
would begin in the new year.

Those states which cannot carry out freightcar repairs
must contribute funds from the budget for their repair,
otherwise they will not be supplied with freightcars in
good condition. Economic and financial levers have
been agreed for those that are the most “disobedient.” It
is also proposed that prices for shipments will be
adjusted, because ultimately everyone suffers from
unwarranted price increases.

There is further good news for the transport industry:
Traffic is resuming on the Veselaya-Sukhumi-Samtredia
line. There is a hope that this will mark the beginning of
the unblocking of the railroads in the Caucasus region:
after all, this affects trade with Turkey and Iran.

Having signed the agreement on further cooperation, the
railroad leaders agreed to meet in April-May next year in
Odessa.

By that time it should be clear what effect all these
warnings and agreements have had. Otherwise that
meeting under the blossoming chestnut trees will end
with so much empty noise.

ITAR-TASS Carries Press Review for 17 Dec

LD1712155794 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 0625
GMT 17 Dec 94

(“Press Review”—ITAR-TASS headline]

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 17 (ITAR-
TASS)—Leading Russian newspapers continue to give
main attention to the situation in and outside Chechnya.
““Moscow has made another attempt to prevent blood-
shed in Chechnya and to settle the conflict in that small
North-Caucasian republic peacefully,”’ writes the
ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA newspaper in its today’s
issue.

The daily notes that “President Boris Yeltsin extended
by another 48 hours the time of laying down arms by all
illegal armed formations in Chechnya”.

The newspaper claims in another article that “the com-
mand (eds: of units of the interior and defence minis-
tries) received information from local population that
panicky sentiments developed among Dudayev’s
gunmen, which flared up into disputes in some cases ard
turned into armed clashes.

“This is attributed to the latest address by the Russian
president to extend by another 48 hours the time of
laying down arms and amnesty.”

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

INTERREGIONAL AFFAIRS

The NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA newspaper features an
appeal by the Chechen diaspora in Moscow to Boris
Yeltsin and Chechen president Dzhokhar Dudayev. The
appeal to Yeltsin says the following: ““Thousands of
Russian people in Chechnya, and even greater numbers
of Chechens live and work in Russia.

“Following long years of joint life when they jointly
experienced much sufferings which fell to the lot of our
peoples, when they are linked by fraternal and friendly
family ties and when the entire economy is built as a
single whole, the geographical position shows that our
peoples cannot be divided by any sovereignties.”

The appeal to Dudayev says: ““Hundreds and even thou-
sands of people who now stand next to you, and even
greater numbers of civilians, can die. The best and the
most honest people die the first. Be it a brief Chechen
war or a large-scale, protracted Caucasian war, the best
part of our people will die’’.

The same newspaper carries an article by Vladimir
Lysenko, chairman of the State Duma (Russian parlia-
ment lower house) subcommittee on the development of
federal relations, who writes: “Now that the level of
bitterness and mistrust between federal executive power
and the Chechen authorities reached its acme, neither
the idea of a constitutional federation, nor the idea of an
independent state can bring an understanding and com-
promise between Russia and Chechnya.

“‘Under these conditions, I believe the idea of a confed-
erative union could be a possible form of compromise.”

The NOVAYA EZHEDNEVNAYA GAZETA news-
paper features an interview with Ingush Vice-President
Boris Agapov where he writes: “‘Following the break-
down of the first negotiations and the start of the troops’
offensive, we have on our hands not a skirmish between
Groznyy and Moscow, between Dudayev and Yeltsin,
but a war between Chechnya and Russia...

“There is one way out of this situation: May be it is more
shameful than earlier but without bloodshedding—to
stop troops.”

“Supporters of two versions for settling the Russian-
Chechen crisis have now taken sides in the leadership of
the republic,” Chechen Vice-President Selimkhan Yan-
darbayev said in an interview with the IZVESTIYA
newspaper.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

““Some believe that the talks in Vladikavkaz were only a
cover for Russia to continue the advance of its armour to
Groznyy. They insist on stepping up combat operations.
However, those who regard the continuation of the talks
the only hope for stopping the bloodshed also preserve
influential positions.

“Chechen Prosecutor-General Usman Imayev is among
them, the newspaper notes. ’He lays the main responsi-
bility for mountains of arms in the republic on the
Russian Army which, leaving Chechnya in 1991, left
there submachine-guns, pistols, tanks, missile launchers,
planes and various ammunition.

“According to Imayev, authorities do not know what to
do with a batch of artillery shells with nuclear warheads.
Imayev claims that the republic does not have a special
depot for this ammunition. They now lie at depots
together with ordinary artillery shells.” The prosecutor-
general claims that the shells are in the area of Shali city.

The same newspaper runs a statement by the Russian
Defence Ministry, refuting that Dudayev’s military
detachments have nuclear weapons. “All our warheads
and bombs are at a right place. We know all of them.
Chechriya had no and does not have nuclear weapons,”
stresses the statement.

The SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA newspaper prints an
interview with Ingush President Ruslan Aushev, saying:
“The bloodshed should be stopped and troops pulled
out. This should not be considered as a defeat or humil-
iation of dignity.

“It is clear that the army will reach Groznyy, will build
up its military might. The country is vast and its might is
great. But does anybody know a historic example when a
people was destroyed?”

““A rapacious sale-out of oil (which is extremely painful
both for Russia and for the west), counterfeiting of
Russian money, forging of false bank advice notes,
piratic attacks on passing trains and formation of ‘trav-
elling’ mobs violated even unwritten code of dishon-
esty,” writes the PRAVDA newspaper.

“Russia saw again like in a mirror what our state may
turn into: Chechnya became, as it were, a dreadful
nrotutype of this. There is no way of avoiding this
ieeling: ‘Xe war in Chechnya became a war with our own
shadow. Therefore, it is just and unjust at the same time.
Hence, our defeats in the battlefield and at the negoti-
ating table...”

Gore Performing ‘Diplomatic Tightrope-Walk’

MM 1612161594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 17
Dec 94 p 4

[Article by Stanislav Kondrashov: “Internal Affair With
International Repercussions’’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Speaking at Wednesday’s [14
December] “roundtable” convened by the Foreign Min-
istry leadership to discuss the results of and possible
prospects for Russian foreign policy, Andrey Kozyrev
covered all the “azimuths” and did not say a word about
Chechnya. Which is understandable: It is an internal
matter and, accordingly, rather more a matter for
[Internal Minister] Viktor Yerin’s department. Never-
theless, as we know, this internal matter has personally
concerned the foreign minister: Was it not because of
Chechnya that Kozyrev left the “‘Russia’s Democratic
Choice” Duma faction and abandoned Gaydar in order
to show his loyalty to Yeltsin?

Chechnya has “plagued” him. The development of
events inevitably raises a broader question: Will it plague
Russia and the Russian president in the international
arena, and how? At first glance, the question has already
been answered: No, it will not.

U.S. President Clinton set the tone by quite clearly
stressing what had been expected, namely that the intro-
duction of Russian troops into Chechnya is Russia’s
internal affair since Chechnya is part of Russia. Vice
President Albert Gore, who is in Moscow for another
reason, backed this idea during his conversations and
talks, and the idea rapidly did the rounds of all the G-7
capitals as far as Tokyo.

However, Clinton’s first response contained another
element that was picked up by his colleagues but kind-of
went unnoticed by us—the hope of minimum bloodshed.
This proviso, which is also natural, harbors the possi-
bility of a chase of approach to events in Chechnya in
the event of mass casualties and devastation, which
would demonstrate the ineffectiveness of the Russian
military machine and thus—in extremely critical cir-
cumstances, moreover—the weakness of the Russian
state and its leadership led by the president. In the eyes
of foreign politicians and the international public
Chechnya constitutes a test for the new Russia, a test to
see whether both its territory and its democracy will fall
apart.

Here are two quotations from lead articles by two British
newspapers, with their unsurpassed experience of cov-
ering military campaigns in multinational states and
empires.

THE TIMES: “A nation cannot tolerate the threat of
armed conflict in its own country. Negotiations backed
by the threat of force would have been the best way out.
But now there is no turning back. If force is to be used,
it must be coordinated and overwhelming. Half-
measures will only increase the opposition and lead to

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

bloodshed. As it is, the political muddle in Moscow and
the abortive military operations in Chechnya make a
swift, effective operation far from likely.

THE DAILY TELEGRAPH: “Although no foreign state
will come to the aid of the insurgents, the Russians’
behavior in Chechnya is a matter of Western concern.
One will be able to judge the nature of the Russian state
from how force is used and how extensively the
Chechens are allowed to enjoy their democratic rights.
And this approach will be an important factor in
defining the West’s positions with regard to Russia on
such matters as the future of Ukraine and the Baltic
states.”

THE NEW YORK TIMES considers Yeltsin’s use of
military force to be “justified”; a writer in France’s LE
MONDE is haunted by the ghost of Afghanistan in
Chechnya and a “new adventure for the Kremlin’s
leaders,” while Germany’s OSTSEEZEITUNG sees the
military expedition as “dancing on a volcano that may
awaken” and believes that “the Caucasus could prove
the end of Yeltsin’s political career.”

Thus journalists, freer with their views, are writing
something that officials prefer to keep quiet for the time
being: Certainly it is an internal matter, certainly we are
not interfering, but we are scrutinizing the situation and
our view of this internal matter will depend on how it
goes. We reserve that right.

In the context of international life the war in Chechnya
began right after the CSCE conference (now renamed the
OSCE) in Budapest. The two buddies—Bill and Boris—
bickered in front of 50 other leaders and the whole
world. In order to ensure that the Cold War did not
become a cold peace, Yeltsin opposed NATO’s expan-
sion via the admission of the USSR’s former allies.
Clinton spoke “in favor” of this proposal and stated that
he does not acknowledge the Kremlin’s right of veto on
this matter

Now the excessive display of military might in Chechnya
along with its hamfisted savagery is providing a trump
card for those countries—NATO candidates—that are
fond of citing the historically proven danger of close
proximity to the “Russian bear”—even the new breed,
which nas not sufficiently proved itself in democratic
society. At the moment this trump card is not being
played so as not to cause excessive embarrassment for
Yeltsin, but it is to hand and may prove useful in time.

On the other hand, the ordinary Chechens who have
gotten into trouble may elicit the compassion of the
civilized world, but this will not be the case with
Dudayev’s regime, which, if it is recognized in the
international arena at all, is only seen as a source of
criminal danger. Even the Muslim world is wary of
unconditionally backing Groznyy’s incumbent ruler.

The fact that the military campaign in Chechnya is
compelling the West to adopt a different position toward

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

4 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

Yeltsin—in the most dramatic circumstances since
October 1993—is equally important. Will they still sup-
port him? Yes, albeit because there is no option. At what
distance should they back him? That needs clarification.

Sober foreign observers at the very least see double when
it comes to the domestic political situation in Russia.
The image of the democrats is divided particularly after
Gaydar, whom the West is accustomed to consider the
incarnation of the Russian democrat, opposed Yeltsin—
the supreme Russian democrat in power—on the ques-
tion of Chechnya.

Cold calculation and quite practical considerations are
forcing Western leaders to side with the man in power,
the man with whom for that reason they can and must do
business. But without the former embraces, without
displays of closeness which the public would not under-
stand given the bloody television and photographic
footage from Chechnya.

While in Moscow Vice President Gore also performed
this diplomatic tightrope-walk. Overly warm relations
with Yeltsin could be perceived as giving the go-ahead
for his accelerated evolution toward authoritarianism.
But gestures of aloofness could be equally dangerous.
They would intensify the impression of the Russian
president’s isolation and would play into the hands of his
nationalist and “neoimperialist’” opponents, who are
also the West’s main adversaries.

After Budapest Washington’s desire to ease the impres-
sion of an impending chill in relations with Moscow is
noticeable. As Kozyrev did at the above “roundtable,”
they are being presented as disagreements within the
partnership, but disagreements that do not cancel it out.

No Progress in Nuclear Sphere Reported at
U.S.-Russia Talks

MK1712082094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 17
Dec 94 p 1

[Report by Pavel Felgengauer in the “Contacts” column:
“The U.S. Market Remains Closed to Russian Uranium.
The Chernomyrdin-Gore Talks Have Not Been as Suc-
cessful as They Have Been Made Out To Be”’}

[FBIS Translated Text] U.S. Vice President Al Gore said
yesterday during a reception at the U.S. Embassy that as
a result of prolonged talks, he and Viktor Chernomyrdin
agreed with the opinion of their wives (Valentina and
Tipper) to change the future time and venue for sessions
of the joint Russian-U.S. commission. The next session
will again take place in Moscow in June, and only the
sixth session will be held in a year in Washington.

Yet it is not so easy to reach agreement on substantive
matters. Atomic Energy Minister Viktor Mikhaylov told
SEGODNYA’s correspondent that no progress has been
reached in the nuclear sphere. “The U.S. market has
remained closed to our uranium: Nobody is going to lift
antidumping decisions.” Moreover, the Americans have

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

unilaterally worsened sharply the terms of an earlier
agreement on supplies of surplus weapons-grade ura-
nium (500 tonnes). ““We have not as yet delivered one
gram under this contract, and under the scheme pro-
posed by the American we shall not implement it at all.
This is unfavorable to us,” Viktor M*khaylov said.
Furthermore, the Americans have not so far allocated
the Atomic Industry Ministry a single cent of the $95
million promised for 1993-94 to reinforce security mea-
sures at nuclear installations. When rhetoric ends and
money business begins, partnership immediately runs
into difficulties.

Summing up the results of the meeting, Viktor Cherno-
myrdin, in a fit of temper, announced that “we are really
tired of 1994. We wish it would end as soon as possible.”

Gore Urges Chernomyrdin To Heed Chevron’s
Interests

MK1612105094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 16
Dec 94 p 1

[Report by Rustam Narzikulov under the “Lobbying”
rubric: “Gore Pursues Chevron’s Kazakhstani Inter-
ests”)

[FBIS Translated Text] According to an ITAR-TASS
report, U.S. Vice President Albert Gore, who is staying
in Moscow, has suggested to Viktor Chernomyrdin that
“international financial organizations be attracted to
appraise the effectiveness of proposals by the U.S. com-
pany Chevron” on joining the Caspian Pipeline Consor-
tium (CPC) to construct an oil pipeline from the Tengiz
field in Kazakhstan to the port of Novorossiysk.

Mr. Gore’s proposal is aimed against the special rights
granted by the Russian Government to Oman, which
will get a 25-percent stake in the CPC in exchange for
$150-$200 million in investment and another $400-$450
million in financial guarantees at the initial stage of the
project. By comparison, Russia and Oman requested
that Chevron pay a one-time bonus worth $300-$400
million and guarantee the minimal utilization of the
pipeline’s capacity in exchange for those same 25 percent
of the shares.

Earlier Chevron refused to accept these terms, but the
company does not have other routes to transport Tengiz
oil but across Russian territory, which explains Albert
Gore’s new initiatives at his working breakfast with
Viktor Chernomyrdin.

Gore, Chernomyrdin Sign Agreements on Space,
Conversion

MM1612165594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 17
Dec 94 p 3

[Report by Sergey Leskov: “‘Nicer To Discuss Space and
Oil To a Blues Accompaniment”’]

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

[FBIS Translated Text] Malicious gossip has it that U.S.
Vice President Albert Gore came to Russia to hear blues
genius B.B. King, who is on tour in Moscow. This is due
to the fact that the results of the visit and the work of the
Russian-U.S. commission on economic, scientific, and
technical cooperation are by no means mind-boggling.
But if you analyze it you find that the apparent slow
progress is due to the extreme complexity of the prob-
lems under discussion.

Special attention is focused on space cooperation. Russia
and the United States are faced with building the mon-
umental “Alpha” space station, which will provisionally
cost $15 billion. In Moscow Gore sounded out the
possibility of putting U.S. astronauts on the station not
in 2002, as previously intended, but in 1998.

It is perfectly possible that Moscow will agree to add one
U.S. astronaut to its crew. The first step has already been
taken in that direction: Chernomyrdin and Gore signed
a memorandum on creating a joint medical and biolog-
ical space research center.

Another two documents were signed in the space sphere.
One of them stipulates the installation of U.S. apparatus
to investigate the ozone layer in the Russian “‘Meteor”
satellite. The second agreement is very interesting. It
concerns a vulgar earthly matter—customs. One tonne of
U.S. scientific equipment for the Russian “Spektr”
module has been sitting doing nothing in Russian cus-
toms. Customs are demanding the payment of a freight
charge, while the Americans claim that it is just passing
th rough Russia on the way into orbit. The agreement
removes customs barriers to space freight.

Experts place the finally agreed plan for the creation of
the first Russian-U.S. joint venture in the conversion
sphere by the Russian ““Nauka” Science and Production
Association and the U.S. “Hamilton Standard” firm (a
subsidiary of “United Technology”) in the category of
minor sensations, which may be followed by cooperation
surpassing the successful space experience by far. At
stage one this venture will develop life-support systems
for civilian aircraft. In March 1995 the first five systems
will be installed on the new Russian Tu-204 aircraft. The
Russian side’s contribution is know-how and production
facilities, with the Americans providing equipment and
money. The cost of the whole project is more than $35
million, and $2.6 million have already been allocated by
the Conversion Enterprises Fund, set up by the U.S.
Department of Defense and the Overseas Private Invest-
ment Corporation.

Accoding to information from informed sources, the
only question on which Gore and Chernomyrdin failed
to achieve mutual understanding was the U.S. “Chev-
ron” Company’s participation in constructing an oil
pipeline from the Tengiz oilfield. On condition that it
receives one-half of the shares, ‘““Chevron” is prepared to
provide the lion’s share of the funding, but the Russian
side regards these proposals as disadvantageous. The

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 5

plan envisages the construction of an oil pipeline from
Tengiz to Novorossiysk and of a freight terminal in the
new Novorossiysk port.

Gore Welcomes Signing

LD1612181694 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1617
GMT 16 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, Dec 16 (INTER-
FAX)—US Vice President Albert Gore highly appreci-
ates the development of the Russian-American coopera-
tion.

Summing up results of the work of the Russian-
American Commission on Economic and Scientific-
Technical Cooperation at a press conference in Moscow,
Gore declared that the parties have managed to create
such an atmosphere in which “any misunderstandings,
which are sometimes inevitable, are directly solved in an
open form.”

Gore pointed to “a great significance” of the documents
which were signed Friday at inter-governmental level.
Among them are the agreement on cooperation to pre-
vent contamination in the Arctic region, on cooperation
in space exploration, on principles of customs registra-
tion of goods shipped in the frameworks of program
providing for cooperation and exploitation of space for
peace purposes.

Among the most important agreements signed at the
level of ministries Gore named the joint statement on
the development of trade-economic cooperation
between Russia’s Far East and America’s western cocst,
the memorandum on mutual understanding between the
Russian government and OPIC corporation to support
conversion in the Russian Federation, and the statement
on financing and insuring investments allotted by OPIC
and amounting to $500 million to implement conversion
projects in Russia.

Gore also reported that both countries would continue
their active work to improve access of Russian goods to
the American market.

The next session of the Chernomyrdin-Gore commission
is to be held in six months’ time in Moscow.

6 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

results of the fourth session of the Russian-U.S. Com-
mission for Economic and Technological Cooperation
which ended in Moscow on Friday [!6 December].

Chernomyrdin stressed that “Russia and America have
learned to build their relations on the basis of respect for
miutual interests and the growing awareness of their
responsibility for the future”.

He said the commission has traversed a path “from
determining the general principles of cooperation to
specific projects which can yield tangible results in the

specific projects the agreement on cooperation in pre-

American agreement on cooperation under the globe
programme, the agreement on customs formalities
relating to goods transported within the framework of
Russian-American cooperation in space research and use
for peaceful purposes.

In the course of the two-day commission session in
Moscow, about twenty major documents were signed
covering different aspects of Russian and U.S. coopera-
tion in the economic, scientific and technological
spheres.

“The work of the fourth session of our commission
showed convincingly that in the eighteen months since
its creation, our joint efforts succeeded in turning it into
an effective mechanism of promoting Russian-American
cooperation in the most sensitive and important spheres
of interaction,” Chernomyrdin said. He pointed to coop-
eration in the spheres of high technology, conversion,
environmental protection and health services.

Chernomyrdin expressed confidence that in a few
months “we shall have another opportunity for meeting
and working productively in the interest of deepening
and developing our partnership”.

Shift in Investment Policy Seen as Gore Visit’s
Main Result

MM1612192594 Moscow KOMSOMOLSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 17 Dec 94 p 3

[Report by Yevgeniy Anisimoyv under the “Visits”
rubric: “Gore Promises the Moon’’}

[FBIS Translated Text] U.S. Vice President Albert Gore
and Russian Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin dis-
cussed the problems of Russian-U.S. cooperation in the
President-Hotel on Thursday [15 December] and Friday.

It is assumed that the fourth session of this commission
should lead to the signing of a number of very important
documents. Notably on developing deposits in the Pri-
obskiy oil field, conversion projects, and cooperation in
space.

According to Yu. Shafranik, Russian minister for fuel
and energy, the main result of the meeting is that
Russia’s foreign partners are moving away from a policy
of offering credits to a strategy of direct investments in
our economy. The advantage of this change is obvious:
Whereas credits have to be repaid, and indeed with
interest, then direct investments mean that the American
side is prepared to share the risk of developing new
projects with us.

Differing U.S. Views Cited on Wider NATO
MM 1612155194 Moscow TRUD in Russian 16 Dec 94
ps

[Commentary by Vissarion Sisnev: ‘‘Moscow-
Washingtcn: Something Has Started To Cool. United
States Predetermines Complication of U.S.-Russian
Relations” }

[FBIS Translated Text] Washington—-The last month of
the year has been marked by the birtin of a new political
term—*“Talbottism.” It means an orientation toward the
strengthening of relations with the new Russia in all
political and economic areas, as championed by Strobe
Talbott, a personal friend of the president and his
adviser on U.S.-Russian problems, who now occupies
the post of deputy secretary of state. Some people have
even claimed that his formula is “‘Russia first.” The first
to use his name as a term was THE WASHINGTON
POST on 11 December, in an article with the expressive
headline “U.S.-Russian Relations Crack,” which said,
citing an unnamed Polish official: ““We can see that
Talbottism has come to an end.”

The article analyzes the broad spectrum of our mutual
relations, but emphasis is put on what occurred at the
meeting of CSCE heads of state and goverament.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 7

Despite the fact that recent days have been full of other
important events, observers continue time and again to
return to the question of extending NATG membership
and of the Russian leaders’ attitude to this. The same
article in the liberal Washington newspaper says that “if
the discord between East and West gets worse, Yeltsin’s
5 December speech at the Budapest summit could be
considered a turning point.” “Europe faces the danger of
beirg plunged into a Cold War,” he said in a speech
colored by suspicion of the West.

It is curious that in both this and most other cases
commentators mention only in passing the differences of
opinion on the Bosnian problem and the second Russian
veto in the Security Council. The main focus of attention
and dissatisfaction is Moscow’s position on NATO’s
future, which was by all accounts predetermined in
Washington political circles long ago, and at the time it
was in no way expected that Moscow’s reaction could be
so distinctly negative. Roughly speaking, it seems that
this future was mapped out as follows: Former USSR
satellites in East Europe simultaneously join the Atlantic
bloc, and West European borders are thus automatically
moved right up to Russia.

Here is what James Baker, Republican foreign policy
ideologist and former secretary of state, writes in an
article circulated by the LOS ANGELES TIMES syndi-
cate: “After the Cold War ended Central European
countries made phenomenal progress toward democracy
and a market system. But Central Europe’s security
remains in _copardy, bearing in mind the potential
instability further to the east. The Clinton administra-
tion’s Partnership for Peace’ proposal predetermined an
inevitable expansion of NATO. The 104th Congress
(now dominated by Republicans—V. S.) must force the
administration to set about expanding NATO without
delay. Specifically, Poland, Hungary, and the Czech
Republic must be accepted as full members of NATO. By
doing this, the United States and their NATO allies will
give a new impetus to the reforms in Central Europe and
will strengthen the continent’s security as a whole.”
Baker, who is seen by many as a future U.S. president,
does not forget about Russia here: “The 104th Congress
must continue funding and technical aid for the reforms
in Russia....”” Further: “Russia is a country that is not
dependent on the United States; our interests sometimes
may not coincide, as happens even with our traditional
allies. But it is important to remember the main thing:
the need to integrate into the international community a
democratic, stable, and trustworthy Russia—the only
country in the world still capable of destroying the
United States.”

But by all accounts, although this was written after
Budapest, Baker is not very concerned about Moscow’s
attitude to the advance toward its borders of a bloc with
which it will have only a distant relationship. Evidently
the former secretary of state also has no doubt that the
Russians will grumble and grumble but nonetheless agree
to everything Washington proposes to them—what else

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

8 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

can they do if this is where the main aid and suppor? for
the reforms is expected to come from? This most likely
explains a remark in THE WASHINGTON POST that
“Secretary of State Christopher is not too concerned by
the Russians’ behavior.”

But the Americans’ viewpoint on NATO expansion and
Russia’s position in this context are by no means con-
fined to the foregoing. It is very indicative that the most
senior of those who were previously called Sovietolo-
gists—Richard Pipes, professor of history at Harvard

and anticommunist when Clinton and Baker were still
going to school, has “sided with the Russians.” He stated
plainly that he quite understands President Yeltsin’s
concern over the proposed expansion of NATO’s sphere
of influence.

Let us assume that Pipes is “out of touch with life” and
that generally his comments do not reach a very wide
circle of people now. Another example can be taken.
SCRIPPS HOWARD NEWS SERVICE political
observer B. Cutler is printed simultaneously in newspa-
pers throughout the country, but he is particularly

His latest column is called ““Reasons for Russia’s Recal-

members to the alliance of 16 nations. This step is not
prompted by necessity and will have the reverse
results.... The Russians have in their blood a historically
shaped fear of being surrounded. They will see a NATO
that is expanding and advancing toward them as a
hostile, dangerous grouping.”

Does this mean that Moscow is scared of its own shadow
and experiencing unjustified fear, Cutler asks his
readers. He answers: It is worth pondering how we would
react if Russia created a “defensive alliance” with
Mexico and started holding joint military maneuvers a
little to the south of El Paso (a Texas city on the border
with Mexico—V. S.). He ends with the advice: “‘Wash-
ington’s ivory-tower strategists should stop muttering
about a new security structure’ for Europe. In diplomacy
the best line is often to leave a given problem alone.”

As the saying goes, the people concerned should take
note.

misunderstanding and cast light upon all issues con-

wher

“Can Russia become a NATO member?” In reply to this

Atlantic alliance chief said “the door is open for Russia
as well,” but if this issue is ever considered the nature of
NATO itself should also be considered. Russia is part of
Europe but the major portion of its territory is in Asia.
Its admission would completely change the basis of the
NATO concept on security. Article 5 of the Washington
Treaty with its “nuclear umbrella” is an insurance police
against any risks. “I do not want to indulge in fantasies,
but when working up the strategy all scenarios should be
taken into consideration,” Claes said. ‘“‘China is not our
neighbor, but it is yours. What if a conflict occurs in the
Far East?”

“I would like to emphasize once again our determination
not to permit Russia to be isolated, not to permit Yalta
to recur, not to divide Europe into ’zones of influence.“
The mistrust resulting from historic causes can somehow
be understood, but otherwise the West’s activities over
the past five years are a goou proof of our goodwill and
our positive approach,” Claes saia.

NATO Secretary General Comments on ‘Cold
Peace,’ Chechnya

LD1712220494 Moscow Ostankino Television First
Channel Network in Russian 1800 GMT 17 Dec 94

[Interview of NATO Secretary General Willy Claes by
Russian Line, Russkaya Liniya, correspondent Umitriy
Khavin in Brussels; date not given—recorded; from the
“Novosti” newscast]

[FBIS Translated Text] [Khavin] Some say that since
NATO’s meeting in December with Foreign Minister
Andrey Kozyrev, we have been living in a state of cold
peace, as they say. Do you agree with this dramatic
assessment of the situation?

[Claes] This expression obviously makes an impression,
but I do not think this. As I have heard, U.S. Defense
Minister Perry is going to Moscow and there may well be
a long meeting over the weekend between Foreign Min-
ister Kozyrev and the U.S. defense minister. There may
aiso be an initiative by NATO that will develop the
dialogue between us, and I am sure that we will continue
it.

[Khavin]} Some analysts believe that NATO’s policy on
Russia is determined by the internal situation there. If
so, then how might the situation in Chechnya and the
political discord in Moscow influence relations between
NATO and Russia.

[Claes] First, this issue was not discussed by the council
or by NATO’s committees, so I am speaking for myself.
First of all, we regard it as internal affairs. The Republic
of Chechnya has never been recognized by the world
community as an independent state. But we are, of
course, worried by what is going on there. We very much
hope that it will be possible to solve the problem without
the use of force and that human rights will be respected.

[Khavin}] How do you see relations between NATO and
Russia in the future? Do you think Russia will become
an equal member of NATO?

[Claes] Frankly, I cannot give an answer simply because
in the internal discussion we are starting on the issue of
NATO’s expansion, we have to answer the question:
How is this organization to be expanded? We are not
asking who should be asked to join or when. So, if you
ask me, for example, if the Czech Republic is to become
a member of NATO, I would not be able to answer. And
I think this is all part of a normal process. Before inviting
one or several countries, you obviously have to deter-
mine the conditions, both for us and for the candidate
country. So let us formulate the answer to what is,
believe me, a very difficult question: How should this be
done? After this we will inform all interested partners. In
other words, this means Russia will be informed of the
conditions for joining. Beyond that, it is the prerogative
of the NATO council to give me a mandate to take the
next steps.

Protocol on Joint Conversion Project With U.S.
Signed

LD1612215094 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 200°
GMT 16 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Aleksey Berezhkov]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 16 TASS—
A protocol on financing of a Russian-U.S. conversion
project was signed here today. The joint venture was
formed by the Russian Nauka scientific-production
amalgamation and the U.S. Hamilton-Standard firm, a
branch of the U.S. United Technologies corporation.

Negotiations on over 100 joint conversion projects have
been on for a long time, sources at the Russian State
Committee for Defence Industries told ITAR-TASS.
However, this is the first time money was allocated for a
specific project.

The joint venture named Hamilton Standard-Nauka is
financing by the Corporation of Private Investments
Abroad and the Fund for Conversion of Enterprises
formed by the U.S. Defence Department. The corpora-
tion and the fund signed the protocol together with the
venture participants. The ceremony was attended by
U.S. Secretary of Defence William Perry who described
the project as a perfect example of joint and mutually
beneficial efforts of the U.S. and Russia in the conver-
sion sphere. The overall cost of the project exceeds 35
million U.S. dollars.

The venture will produce systems for civil planes,
including air- conditioning installations. Russian scien-
tific projects will be used alongside U.S. technological
equipment. Russian air-conditioning systems for planes
exceed the world class on many parameters and are
cheaper than the U.S. and European analogues by 20 per
cent. The products will be used in Russian II-96, Tu-204
and Tu-334 planes of new generation. Negotiations with
Boeing on possible delivery of the systems are underway.

President of the Corporation of Private Investments
Abroad Ruth Harkin welcomed the Russian-U:S. project
and said she has signed a statement on financing and
insurance of investments at an amount of up to 500
million U.S. dollars for conversion projects in Russia
and other Commonwealth states within the framework
of a recent session of the Russian-U.S. commission for
economic and technological cooperation.

counterpart Pavel Grachev because he was in Chechnya.

isolation of Moscow. Perry not just once repeated this
point in his negotiations with Kokoshin. Generally, the
Pentagon head put a thrust not on disagreements but

amount to $32 million. The joint veriure will put out its
first products in the first quarter of 1995.

‘Sources’ Cited on Russian Arms Deals With Iraq

LD1912110094 London THE INDEPENDENT in
English 19 Dec 94 p 10

[Report by Adel Darwish: “Moscow Poised to Rearm
Iraqis’’}

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow is ready to resume
arms supplies to Iraq once United Nations sanctions
have been lifted, according to Russian and Iraqi sources.

Last month the Russian Foreign Minister, Andrey
Kozyrev, signed a big arms and military-equipment deal
with Iraq’s deputy Prime Minister, Tariq ’Aziz. This
followed an unpublicised meeting in July between Mr
Kozyrev and Mr ’Aziz in St. Petersburg, attended by
senior military and intelligence officers, and extensive
contacts between the two sides in Switzerland, Austria,
Cyprus as well as Iraq and Russia.

The Russians are to supply Baghdad with heavy equip-
ment to replace armour, air defences and radar systems
destroyed during the Gulf war.

The deal also includes modernising Iraq’s forces and
rebuilding airbases.

Groups of Iraqi officers from the Air Defence Corps are
to be trained in Russia for work on communications and
signal intelligence. Russia was President Saddam
Husayn’s main arms supplier following the signing of a
friendship treaty in 1971, but in the late 1980s the
French began to compete and overtook the Russians in
supplying air-defence systems, naval hardware and air-
craft.

Iraq has agreed to give Russia a “preferential trade status
and priority payment” before other suppliers, as well as
starting to pay for the new contracts as soon as the UN
sanctions are lifted and Iraqi oil sales are allowed again.
“This is important,” said one Russian source, “because
Russia is now at the top of the list of Iraqi debtors.”

More than 20 foreign countries are owed several billion
dollars by the Iraqis for projects abandoned after the
Iraqi invasion of Kuwait in 1990. The Russians signed
another multi-million-dollar contract with Baghdad in
August to resume oilfield and refinery projects which
were interrupted by the sanctions.

Arms Sales to China, Kuwait Raise Questions
MK1612140994 Moscow NOVAYA
YEZHEDNEVNAYA GAZETA in Russian 16 Dec 94
pp 1, 2

[Report by Viktor Yershov in the “Military Secret”
column: “Kuwait and China: Brothers in Arms. Russian
Arms” }

{[FBIS Translated Text] The recent visit by the Chinese
defense minister to Russia received little coverage in the
mass media. For the most part information about it was
rather general and unspecific.

RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS 11

Meanwhile some fairly intriguing details have filtered
through from responsible sources in the Russian Feder-
atioi. Defense Ministry. Few will know that Colonel
General Khaotyan {as transliterated] was met at the
airport by Army Gen. Konstantin Kobets, deputy Rus-
sian defense minister; and Col. Gen. Anatoliy Kley-
menov, deputy chief of the General Staff. This was
clearly a “retaliation” to the Chinese for according
Grachev during his latest visit to China a “mid-level”
reception. All this obviously suggests that not all is well
in relations between the two countries. Military analysts
believe that this is due not only to the repercussions of
the ’seventies, but also the new moves made by Russia in
the military sphere. This primarily relates to the Russian
Federation’s joining the NATO Partnership for Peace
program, which arouses great concern in China. It is far
from accidental that this past spring Col. Gen. Kolesni-
kov, General Staff chief, visited Beijing, where the
receiving side clearly indicated that it will not feel very
“comfortable” having its longest state border (4,000 km)
with a neighbor which has allicd with NATO. And that
they intend to draw the most serious conclusions from
this.

It was no accident therefore that the main question at the
negotiations between Grachev and Khaotyan was the
signing of an intergovernment agreement on preventing
military activity. The document provides for averting
incidents between the two armies: border crossing by
warplanes and battleships, accidental missile launches,
and so forth. The Chinese are also concerned over
Russia’s plans to build the Svobodnyy-17 cosmodrome a
mere 40 km frcm the Chinese-Russian border. The
question of the DPRK was also raised.

The Chinese minister indicated that his country will not
accept any power methods: a direct hint at U.S. pressure
on Pyongyang. Because China has not just once, directly
and indirectly, expressed solidarity with the North
Korean regime in its confrontation with the United
States, it was very difficult for Grachev to maneuver on
this delicate subject, and so he made vague and inco-
herent statements.

On the other hand, it was with tremendous interest that
the Russian Federation defense minister discussed with
his Chinese counterpart the sale of Russian weapons and
spare parts to China. This concerned tanks, fighter
aircraft, air defense systems, and some types of warships.
The Chinese buy Russian weapons willingly because they
are cheaper and better than many U.S., German, and
British models. At present China’s 3.5-million-strong
army is being actively rearmed, and therefore the local
arms market is virtually insatiable. On one hand, Russia
seeks to gain a foothold on this market, but on the other,
according to confidential sources, the Russian Govern-
ment is in doubt: Are we not arming such a menacing
neighbor to our own detriment? In this situation General
Staff analysts are quite puzzled: In the West we are
seeking to take refuge under the NATO wing, but in the
East we are arming the world’s largest army, which has
no sympathies for the North Atlantic bloc.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

12 RUSSIA INTERNATIONAL AFFAIRS

True, Pavel Sergeyevich [Grachev] trades not only with
China. After Kuwaiti Defense Minister Shaykh Ahmad
al-Hamid al-Jabir al- Sabah visited Moscow, where he
signed a protocol on collaboration in the security sphere
with Grachev, a scandal erupted in the emirate parlia-
ment. In particular over some shady aspects of the
Russian- Kuwaiti deal involvin~ the sale of a large
consignment of arms, combat hardware, and various
military equipment. The alarm was raised by parliament
deputy Nasr als-Sani’a, who had come by information
that not everything about the arms transaction was legal.
As a result, the parliament decided to hold special
hearings on this matter, and the Kuwait defense minister
was confronted with a package of rather embarrassing
questions, the main one being: Could negotiations on
arms procurements be conducted directly with the Rus-
sian Federation Defense Ministry and its leadership? At
the time, under President Yeltsin’s edict, negotiations
could be held only by representatives of the state com-
pany Rosvooruzheniye. Another question is the seller’s
commission (15 percent). According to information in
the deputy’s possession, “other government sources have
analogous equipment, but at much cheaper prices: 40
percent below those asked from Kuwait.” Another inter-
esting detail: Apparently the equipment has been sitting
at the depots of the Warsaw Pact countries, was manu-
factured a long time ago, and is inferior to modern
state-of-the-art models.

no incidents between ships and aircraft during the year

taken it back under the agreement concluded between
the former Soviet Union and the Czechoslovak
Republic.

Yeltsin Sets up Chechnya Plenipotentiary

LD1912114594 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1141
GMT 19 Dec 94

{FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, Dec 19 (INTER-
FAX)}—President Boris Yeltsin has issued a decree on
the appointment of his own plenipotentiary to the
Republic of Chechnya.

The plenipotentiary is described as an official repre-
senting the Russian president in Chechnya. He is to be
appointed or dismissed by the Russian president and is
personally accountable to him.

The plenipotentiary is to take part in coordinating activ-
ities of the federal and regional governments aimed at
normalizing the situation and restoring law and order to
the Chechen republic.

He is “entitled to take part in the work of executive
bodies in the Chechen republic and make proposals on
draft acts to the Russian president and federal govern-
ment covering the issues of normalization” in that
republic and the North Caucasian region as a whole.

The decree came into effect from Sunday when it was
signed, Yeltsin’s press service said.

Vice Premier Nikolay Yegorov now represents the Rus-
sian president in Chechnya.

Russian Democracy Undergoes ‘Test’ in Chechnya

MM1912101594 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 16
Dec 94 p4@

[Article by IZVESTIYA Analysis Center under the gen-
eral heading “Lessons of the Chechen Crisis” and the
“Express Analysis of the Week” rubric: “Simple Test for
Democracy”]

[FBIS Translated Text] General Yermolov called the
Caucasus an enormous fortress. Prerevolutionary
statesman Vasiliy Velichko called it an enormous
academy. Present-day Speaker Shumeyko, with
Chechnya in mind, called it a cancerous tumor. It
appears that military-political surgery by the heirs of
Yermolov, Velyaminov, von Kluegenau, and Gurko has
overlooked the experience of many centuries of storming
the Caucasus fortress, and has approached it ignoring the
extensive lessons taught at the Caucasus academy.

Events in Chechnya, dubbed the Chechen crisis, have
exposed signs of a crisis in Russian statehood. As our
more perspicacious ancestors wrote, “the black hole of
the Caucasus has taken on a new form, maybe a fateful
form, or maybe an ultimately useful one, by exposing the
internal ulcers of our life, the infirmities of our spirit,
and the mistakes and sins of our policy toward the
backwaters.”

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

It feels somehow shameful to even talk about useful-
ness—because of the blood that has been shed. Never-
theless, in the interests of the future, some kind of
lessons must be drawn from the events of the past week.

First Lesson: Chronological

When planning the “introduction of servicemen” in a
particular region of the Federation, the calendar should
be consulted. Any day of the week is fine for launching
an expedition, but the eve of a national holiday deserves
preference. The president’s Press Secretary Vyacheslav
Kostikov noted that there is .>ither a “war party” nora
“peace party” in the Kremlin, there is only the “party of
the Constitution.” Therefore, the “party of the Consti-
tution” chose the introduction of troops into Chechnya
as the best out of the multitude of methods for marking
this red letter day in the calendar [Constitution Day, 12
December].

Second Lesson: Ethnological

When planning a trip to the Caucasus, it is useful to
consult experts on the Caucasus. In order to avoid
possible misunderstandings as regards hasty statements
on the ethnic mentality of the “adversary.”” For example,
his favorite habit of building a protective wall using
women, the elderly, and children.

“Phenomena of life and human nature in the Caucasus,”
a certain Caucasus expert from the last century wrote,
“are extremely vivid. They are of particular value to
representatives of the science of statecraft, so little
prevalent—alas!—even in our ruling classes, for whom it
is high time to tackle their own duties more conscien-
tiously....”

Third Lesson: Informational

“The operation to blockade Groznyy” is not “Desert
Storm,” but it is not a storm in a teacup either. It is
entirely capable of shaking the foundations of the Rus-
sian state. No matter what you call it, the “‘small victo-
rious war” in the Caucasus needed a small victorious
verbal campaign. In which those who are supposed to be
in charge would speak, and speak in a coordinated,
rather than discordant, way. In our case we do not know
who is in charge, but ~e can hear who is doing the deed.
But again, it is unclear whether they are in charge.

For post-surgery reasons, the president has restricted
himself to written statements. The premier was silent for
three days, and when he did open his mouth, it was
frightening. He was plain terrifying. The gathering of
aides relaying the president’s opinion was not complete,
but those who were there were completely at odds.
Mythical analysts from various press services shuffled
their personal suppositions. The Security Council is
silent. The unified state position—which may turn out
not to exist at all—has been buried under the splinters
and fragments of “information-analytical and propa-
ganda backup.” There are the individual opinions of
individual leaders of individual departments.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

As always, it is the truth that is dying first in this war too.
Journalists (with the exception of the committed ones)
have been deprived of the professional privilege of
drawing information from primary sources, but on the
other hand they have acquired the privilege of obtain:g
socks on the jaw, punches, and severe injuries from the
tank trail-blazers.

Fourth Lesson: Domestic

The “‘Chechen crisis” has reshuffled the Russian polit
ical pack in a most intricate way. Who could have
imagined that Gaydar and Ampilov would be on one
side of the political barricades, and Fedorov and Zhiri-
novskiy on the other? This unexpected and sometimes
apathetic drift of positions shows that the crisis is
something more than an attempt to settle a particular
problem in one corner of our vast Russia.

Analyzing the positions of political parties and State
Duma factions, it can be noted that those who are
fundamental opponents of the reform process and will
definitely be running against B. Yeltsin in the upcoming
presidential elections were the people who expressed
approval for the actions in Chechnya. And condemna-
tion came from those who have more or less supported
the government’s line and were ready to support the
president in his attempt (if such an attempt were to
follow) to run for a second term.

If we move away from the geopolitical, ethnic, economic,
legalistic, criminal, and other aspects of the Chechen
problem, then the following model takes shape: The
executive has undertaken actions which are to a great
extent capable of influencing the life of the entire
country. But we should not ignore the question: What is
society’s degree of influence on the adoption of such
decisions?

In other words, Chechnya has become a touchstone for
the maturity of the state’s democratic institutions. Just
like Chernobyl in the past served as a touchstone for
glasnost and the informational openness of society. The
test at that time produced a negative result. What will the
Chechnya test show?

The generally accepted pattern is known: The executive
adopts serious decisions in agreement with the people.

When tested, this proved to be a myth. The decision to
send troops in was adopted without any coordination
with the legislature. In other words, it turned out that our
executive power has the potential to carry out actions
with far-reaching consequences without these actions
being approved by the country’s population via their
representatives in the Federal Assembly.

It is accepted to believe that political structures express
the opinion of the people. In our current conditions it is
impossible to clearly assess the reliability of this thesis,
since the process of the formation of the positions of
political forces in respect of the events taking place in
Chechnya is not yet finished. Take as example the

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 15

evolution of the position of Russia’s Democratic Choice
party which, following an initial very painful reaction
(accompanied by rallies and demands for the impeach-
ment of the president) found itself on the brink of a split
(compare the statements of the chairmen of the party’s
Political Council and Executive Committee) but then
managed to elaborate a more moderate compromise:
decision.

An analysis of the positions of State Duma parties and
factions shows that considerations which are far
removed from a quest for less painful methods of unrav-
eling the “Chechen knot” are exerting considerable in‘lu-
ence on their formation. An example is offered by the
position of the Liberal Democratic Party of Russia,
which has to all intents and purposes granted an indul-
gence for any actions by the executive.

The feedback operating in democratic society—the
opinion of citizens expressed in the positions of political
parties, public movements, and representatives of the
people—should have an influence on the actions of the
authorities

The question of the reliability of this thesis in Russian
conditions also remains open—the president has
instructed the Federal Assembly to express its opinion,
the assembly has done so, but the tank columns have
continued to advance.

Chernomyrdin Leads Kremlin Meeting

LD1912103494 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1900
GMT 19 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 19 ITAR-
TASS—Members of the Russian Cabinet of Ministers
and of president’s Security Council and administration
are conferring in the Kremlin with Prime Minister
Victor Chernomyrdin to review approaches to the settle-
ment in and around Chechnya where an extremely tense
impasse has taken shape after Chechen leader Dudayev
rejected yet another goodwill gesture of President
Yeltsin’s.

The present situation is seen in Moscow as fraught with
very grave consequences.

On Sunday, Yeltsin proposed in a telegram to Dudayev
that the latter meet “without delay” Russian Vice-
Premier Nikolay Yegorov and Federal Counterintelli-
gence Chief Sergey Stepashin in Mozdok in order to sign
on the same day a document on ceasefire and on turning
in all weapons.

Moscow also sent out to Dudayev other signals of
peaceful nature. Thus the Russian president’s Chief of
Staff Sergey Filatov told a press conference in Moscow
on Sunday afternoon that “if Dudayev gives a non-
nonsense order to his supporters to lay down arms
immediately, the settlement of the Chechen crisis will
again return to a peaceful path”.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

16 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

However, by the end of Sunday, Dudayev had sent the
Russian president a message stating only that he was
“ready to receive in Groznyy an authorised representa-
tive of the Russian president”. The Chechen leader then
said at a press conference that the Chechen people “will
not allow him to meet Yegorov (Russia’s nationalities
minister) or anyone else (of that rank)”.

Russia’s First Vice-Premier Oleg Soskovets told the
Russian government’s operational headquarters on
Sunday that “no attempts at starting actual talks can be
traced on Dudayev’s part’. According to Soskovets,
Groznyy was trying “to procrastinate in any possible way
in the hope to win time for consolidating its supporter
both in and outside Chechnya”.

All this makes clear that Chechen leader Dzhokhar
Dudayev appear to have actually “‘burnt down all
bridges” on the way leading towards negotiations and
the settlement of the conflict in the republic by peaceful
means and opted for armed resistance to Moscow’s
actions aiming to restore constitutional law and order in
the Chechen republic.

In this context, a spokesman for the president told
ITAR-TASS that “there will be no more replies to
Dzhokhar Dudayev’s telegrams”.

The Sunday night and the early hours of Monday saw no
large- scale military action in Groznyy or Chechnya as a
whole. The TASS correspondent in Groznyy, Russian
planes flew over Groznyy at 02.00 [as received] and dealt
an airstrike at some targets. The explosions were clearly
audible in central Groznyy and window glass was shat-
tered in residential buildings close to where the ITAR-
TASS bureau is located. ITAR-TASS has information
that two missiles exploded in the city area where the
houses of Dzhokhar Dudayev and his relatives are situ-
ated. There was no immediate report about casualties.

While the Russian leaders are debating Moscow’s further
moves with relation to Chechnya, opinions in the
country and beyond differ vastly. It is difficult to state
with certainty which way the political barometer is
pointing, but it is clear that despite the danger of grave
consequences from a forcible solution to the conflict in
Chechnya, many people are in favour of the use of force
in this case.

The council of atamans (chieftains) of the union of
Cossacks of Russia, in particular, has called on the
Russian leadership to solve the Chechen crisis by
forceful means and proposed that the Cossack military
formations be used for the purpose. “‘We demand that
force be used in full measure against Dudayev’s bandit
forces,” the union of Cossacks of Russia said in a
statement addressed to President Boris Yeltsin and
received by ITAR-TASS on Sunday. The statement is
signed by the head of the union of Cossacks Alesandr
Martynov and nine leaders of the regional Cossack
formations in the Northern Caucasus.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Russia must protect its state interests in Chechnya and
its territorial integrity, Georgian leader Eduard Shevard-
nadze said in his speech at the repubiican conference of
state security officers on Sunday.

According to Shevardnadze, should Russia fail to curb
Chechnya’s aggressive separatism, a process of disinte-
gration of the Russian state might begin, which will
affect the development of events in the whole world.

Filatov Sees Possibility for Talks

LD1812183194 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1711
GMT 18 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, Dec 18 (Interfax)—
The chief of staff of the Russian president Sergey Filatov
believes that the talks with Dzhokhar Dudayev are
possible, if the Chechen leader orders that his supporters
surrender fire arms. Addressing in Moscow on Sunday a
briefing, Filatov said that now on the agenda are not
negotiations, but the disarmament of illegal armed for-
mations in Chechnya. Filatov said that the Dudayev
regime is attempting to replace disarmament by the
talks, and these are different things.

He said that throughout Saturday Dudayev three times
ignored invitations to arrive for the talks in Mozdok,
while his telegram to Boris Yeltsin about his prepared-
ness for the talks delayed for several hours operations to
disarm illegal units after the deadline of the president's
ultimatum expired. Groznyy’s preparedness for the talks
“is not confirmed by ceasefire”. Groznyy did not fulfil
the promise to withdraw the armed detachments for one
kilometer. As a result “our people were trapped and
sustained losses”.

Answering the question put by Interfax, Filatov said that
the Russian president “staying in hospital, keeps the
situation in control”. He said that doctors recommended
that Yeltsin stay in hospital to continue medical treat-
ment and the recent meeting of the Security Council was
held there.

Speaking about hostilities in Chechnya, Filatov said that
“those are not war, those are military operations”. He
said that operations of the Russian army and interior
forces in Chechnya are “a special type of operations”,
during which “militants are forced to surrender fire arms
and military technology” and no harm is being dealt to
the civilian population. Fle said that the federal author-
ities have no experience tc conduct “such kind of special
operations”.

Answering the question on how effective Russian special
services oppose the penetration to Chechnya of “mili-
tants” from other states, Filatov said: ““Bad opposition,
we have no successes in this sphere”’.

He said that the morale of the troops deployed in
Chechnya is “normal”. “It changes the way as the
sentiments change within society as a whole”, said

Cossacks Call For Force, Offer Help

LD1812164894 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1622
GMT 18 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 18 TASS—
Russian Cossack leaders urged the government to
resolve the Chechen crisis by force and offered their
help.

“‘We demand that force be used in full against (Chechen
President Dzhokhar) Dudayev’s illegal armed bands,” a
statement adopted by the union of Russian cossacks on
Sunday [18 December] said.

The statement, signed by Cossacks’s Union leader
Aleksandr Martynov and the leaders of nine regional
Cossack divisions, was sent to President Boris Yeltsin.

The Cossack leaders suggest setting up three battalions of
Terek Cossacks to ensure, in cooperation with Russian
law enforcement bodies, “constitutional order, guard
economic facilities, fight illegal armed groups and
destroy them.”

The Cossacks’ Union supports the decisions with regard
to Chechnya adopted by the president and government
and confirms that “the Cossacks are ready to act as the
guardians of peace and constitutional order in the North
Caucasus,” the statement said.

“In connection with the possible use of force to imple-
ment the set tasks of restoring legality and law and order
and ensuring basic human rights and liberties on the
territory of the Chechen Republic as a component part of
the Russian Federation, it is hereby announced that
force will be employed with due consideration of the
principle of humaneness.

“All possible measures are being taken and will be taken
to ensure that there are no casualties among the peaceful
population, but force will be used against those who put
up armed resistance to the legitimate demands of the
authorities.

to guarantee their departure from territories adjacent to
facilities against which force will evidently be used.

“Persons who do not submit to the demands of the
authorities and who put up armed resistance to them,
should ensure the evacuation of the peaceful population
- of women, old people, children and of all who are not
involved in their actions.

“Unless that is done, those who do not fulfill this
generally accepted humane demand will be regarded as

peaceful population and will bear the fitting responsi-
bility.

Federation Council Adopts Chechnya Resolution

LD1712185694 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1551 GMT 17 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Lyudmila Aleksan-
drova]

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 17 Dec—The Federa-
tion Council has proposed that Boris Yeltsin should take
measures to put an immediate end to combat operations
by all sides and continue the process of government
delegations’ talks at a higher level, proceeding from the
principles of preserving the integrity of the Russian
Federation and of granting the opportunity for the free
expression of the will of the people of the Chechen
Republic. The deputies proposed that the Russian pres-
ident should address them on the issue of settling the
situation in Chechnya.

These proposals were set out in the resolution of the
upper chamber of the Russian Parliament, which
adopted the final version of the resolution “On the
situation in the Chechen Republic” at today’s evening
sitting.

The Federation Council also decreed in accordance with
the Constitution of the Russian Federation that all
warring sides of the Chechen Republic should disarm
immediately all illegal military formations and cease any
provocative actions.

The document, approved after a number of substantial
amendments were introduced to the text, which was
adopted earlier as a basis for further discussion, assesses
the situation that has evolved in the Chechen Republic
since the adoption of the resolution of the Federation
Council of 8 December 1994 “On the situation in the
Chechen Republic” and also the appeal by the Russian
president to Federal Assembly deputies.

The resolution notes that the Russian president and
government are taking measures to normalize the situa-
tion in the Chechen Republic and around it, including
those regulating the activity of the federal bodies of state
power to defend the territorial integrity and security of
the Russian Federation and the rights and freedoms of
citizens. At the same time, no real progress has been
achieved yet in resolving the crisis.

The Federation Council drew attention to the fact that
the Russian president and government did not take into
account in good time a number of recommendations set
forth in the resolution of the Federation Council of 8

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

20 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

December 1994, in particular, clause number 2 on the
non-use of force on the territory of the Chechen Republic
until the adoption, in accordance with the Russian
Constitution, of another resolution by competent bodies.

The deputies supported the proposal by the Russian
president on setting up a supervision commission
[nablyudatelnyy sovet] for monitoring the observation of
the constitutional rights and freedoms of citizens while
the resolutions on restoring constitutional order on the
territory of the Chechen Republic are being imple-
mented. It has been decided to include in this commis-
sion 12 representatives of the Federation Council,
among whom are two deputy chairmen of the upper
chamber—Ramazan Abdulatipov and Anatoliy Dogo-
laptev.

The chamber instructed the appropriate committees to
draw up proposals to regulate by law the use of Russian
Armed Forces on the territory of the Russian Federation.

Army have frightened our people. The chaos is artificial;

Vasilyev Becomes Chubays New Deputy

MK1712124094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 17 Dec 94 p 2

[Report by Leonid Brodskiy: “The First Vice Premier
Has Received Apparatus Support”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] Viktor Chernomyrdin’s consent
to approve Dmitriy Vasilyev as deputy chairman of the
Federal Commission on Securities and the Stock Market
is a routine event owing to its predictability. To analyze
the real lineup of “‘economic forces” in the White house,
however, it is more interesting to consider two govern-
ment decrees issued yesterday: ““On the Federal Com-
mission on Securities and the Stock Market of the
Russian Federation” and “On the Government Com-
mission on Financial and Monetary-Credit Policy.”

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 23

That Dmitriy Vasilyev will become one of Anatoliy
Chubays’ deputies in the government Federal Commis-
sion on Securities and the Stock Market was obvious
almost the moment this new body was set up. The
government officials’ confidence stemmed not only from
the fact that Chubays and Vasilyev have been working
together even since they were in Leningrad. Dmitriy
Vasilyev, in spite of his young—by apparatus stan-
dards—age [32] has already shown himself as an experi-
enced polemicist and tough administrator.

In the very near future he will apparently have to call on
these qualities to a greater extent: The formation of the
commission as a government body has entered a final
stage. Under a decree signed by the premier, the Federal
Commission on Securities is given all the trappings of a
full-fledged ministry (except the premises: The address
of the commission headquarters will become known
within two weeks). The commission includes 22 top
government officials—from First Vice Premier Anatoliy
Chubays (commission chairman) to the deputy justice
minister. Experience shows, however, that without a
technical apparatus no power body can lay claim even to
tertiary roles in the political-economic hierarchy of gov-
ernment structures. A 117-member executive board will
formed within the federal commission, which will effec-
tively provide Chubays with real power levers.

Thus Anatoliy Chubays—Viktor Chernomyrdin’s first
economic deputy—has managed substantially to revise
the status of the cabinet’s economic policy ideologue:
Unlike, say, Yegor Gaydar, his chances of fully imple-
menting his intentions are immeasurably higher owing to
the existing apparatus support. This point is also borne
out by the decree on reforming the credit commission
created back under Boris Fedorov, ~vhich will also be
headed by Chubays. In addition to broader powers, the
jurisdiction of this body ‘vill include the entire range of
government financial and budget-credit policy: The
commission will also have a working group whose
chairman is Deputy Finance Minister Andrey Kazmin.

Fund Shortage Slows Komi Clean-Up

LD1812061994 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1748 GMT 17 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Viktor Makarov]

[FBIS Translated Text] Usinsk (Komi Republic), 17 Dec
(TASS)—Another commission concerning the accidents,
which occurred in August-September on the oil pipeline
near Usinsk, today ended its work here. It consisted this
time of members of a commission of deputies of the
State Duma, Ministry of Environment, and the Socio-
Ecological Association of Russia.

None of the numerous commissions which have been
here have yet given any real help especially in resolving
the problems of finance of the work. There is a cata-
strophic shortage of money for the work of cleaning up
territory and building a new oil pipeline.

The people working here have not even received their
wages for months now. The 100-billion-ruble loan prom-
ised by the Ministry of Fuel and Energy has been
obstructed for more than a month now by officials in
Moscow.

Aid of 150 million rubles allegedly allocated by the
Ministry of for Emergencies has, despite its insignifi-
cance, not been received either.

A telegram requesting the allocation of 45 billion rubles
aid for eliminating the aftermath of the accidents was
sent to Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin by head of
the Komi Republic Yuriy Spiridonov and this has so far
been left unanswered.

The rate of work cleaning up polluted territory and
building a new pipeline remains extremely slow.
