Government Discusses State Contol of Ostankino

MM1612132394 Moscow RABOCHAYA TRIBUNA in
Russian 16 Dec 94 p 7

{EFIR-DAYDZHEST report: “Ostankino. What a
Dainty Morsel!”’]

(FBIS Translated Text] Passions have been aroused over
Ostankino which has been transformed by presidential
edict into a public television company [obshchestven-
noye televideniye}. Well-informed sources close to the
government assert that some government structures are
actively discussing the idea of assigning the controling
stake in the public television company to a single state
organization. According to that same source, this orga-
nization could be either the Federal Service for Televi-
sion and Radio Broadcasting or the Committee for
Emergency Situations.

It has also come to light from informed sources that the
Russian president has given written approval to the list
of shareholders in the public television company. There
are four state enterprises among whom the controling
stake will be distributed—Ostankino, the Television
Technical Center, ITAR-TASS, and the State Committee
for the Management of State Property. Of the 51 percent
stake, 9 percent of the shares will go to Ostankino. The
State Committee for the Management of State Property
will receive a large share.

In addition to state enterprises the shareholders will
include eight other organizations. The association of
independent program producers will be represented by
RN-TV, VID, and Avtorskoye Televideniye. The
founder banks will apparently include Menatep, Inko-
mbank, Avtovazbank, and the Stolichnyy Bank.

Results in Various Concerns

MM1612142194 Moscow IZVESTIYA in Russian 16
Dec 94 p 9

{Article by Nikolay Serebryakov: “The Monster Is
Dying. Long Live the Monster?”’]

[FBIS Translated Text] So, “Ostankino” is to become
the “Public Russian Television” [PRT] Joint-Stock
Company. The president has decided this. According to
his edict, it is to broadcast in just over one month’s time.
As is known, 51 percent of the shares remain with state
structures such as the “Ostankiro” Television and Radio

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyrigs.: owners

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Broadcasting Company, the State Committee for the
Management of State Property, ITAR-TASS, and the
Television Technical Center.

But suddenly...information is received from sources that
are perfectly trustworthy: The head of the Russian Fed-
eration Government has an updated draft on his desk. It
provides for the transfer of those same 51 percent of the
shares to the Federal Service for Television and Radio
Broadcasting [FSTRB]. And yet it did not originally
feature at all among the shareholders. This is under-
standable, since it has neither tangible property nor
property rights. What is it doing here?

What is its purpose? Whom is it to control? Oleg
Poptsov? You cannot particularly order him about. The
regional television companies? They are obstinate and
shy away from FSTRB tutelage, as the devil shies away
from incense.

The service is losing functions. But the chief thing is the
possibility of holding sway.

It is hard to assume that the premier of the government
fostered the new draft, for the state holds the controlling
package, and there are no discernible reasons for
entering into direct conflict with the president, who
approved the original scheme for distributing the shares.

Aleksandr Yakovlev, head of the FSTRB, does not have
such reasons either: Rumor ascribes precisely to him the
idea of creating public television. It is naive to think that
an experienced democratic market politician was reck-
oning on creating a television company with the partic-
ipation of private capital, which would be controlled by
the state. Particularly as Aleksandr Nikolayevich
becomes chairman of the PRT Board of Directors, a still
more important and responsible figure and, moreover,
independent of the political struggle.

Who, in that case, has an interest in the innovation? The
authors—quite obviously!—should be sought within the
FSTRB itself.

Created exactly a year ago, bui so far having failed to
convince society that it is needed, this service remains to
this day an example of a bureaucratic, protracted con-
struction project. It has only just started to be staffed.
Until recently it consisted of the leader, A. Yakovlev,
and...V. Lazutkin, his first deputy. We will not list the
latter’s recent “services.” Suffice it to recall that it was
Lazutkin who virtually headed the memorable coverage
of the election campaign in December 1993, when Mr.
Zhirinovskiy’s monologues dominated the airwaves and
the shamefully noisy party in the Kremlin Palace was
given airtime. Mr. Lazutkin was dismissed but then...ap-
pointed first deputy at the FSTRB. He is still in the same
office. He remains the most influential figure in the
television and radio company. With the emergence of
PRT he loses everything. But what if the FSTRB receives
51 percent of the shares? Then Valentin Valentinovich
will be able to “rule the roost” in television on behalf of
the state.

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 25

It can be assumed that such a course of events would
benefit those who are interested in beefing up state
control over television coverage of events in Chechnya.
For Mr. Lazutkin’s many years’ professional experience
in the External Relations Administration of the Gostel-
eradio [State Committee for Television and Radio
Broadcasting] (we know what else the journalists whom
it watched over did and where they frequently served!)
enables us to hope that he will not let down the sup-
porters of a tough policy, like the one now being pursued
in Chechnya.

And yet it is not a question of precisely who will be the
biggest chief of the structure which holds the controlling
package of shares. The main point is that such a depart-
ment, which has no aims other than to order the elec-
tronic mass media about and which lives for this, its
monopoly function, will bury the very idea of public
television in Russia. In addition, there will be a sharp
diminution of the effect expected from admitting private
capital into television. And yet precisely this is at the
basis of the idea of public television, and precisely this
will make it possible to recoup the cost and make quality
programs.

But the option that involves placing 51 percent of the
PRT shares in the hands of a single state structure—an
option in which they are trying to interest even V.
Chernomyrdin, who is a champion of the market—
cancels everything out and turns PRT into a simple
appendage of the state department. Instead of public
television, there will be the same old Gosteleradio
headed by officials. It is suggested to private investors:
“Paying money is up to you. But what we do with it is up
to us.”

An official is an official. His function is not to save
money, still less to make it. His function is to spend
money. There is no need to demonstrate that this art has
been brought to perfection in the state’s “Ostankino.” If
the new option for a share flotation prevails, private
companies most likely will not play such games. And
then two state television companies again? And again the
question—where is the money to be found?

This will no longer be public television but, according to
the old demagogic formula, television of “the whole
people.” This means television of officialdom....

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Aleksandr Yakov-
levich, will society be able to survive without losses the
recent liberalization of the prices of many goods,
including fuel prices? Especially since the state intends in
return to allocate how much oil should be delivered to
whom within the country.

[Livshits] The question goes far deeper. It is a question
of the kind of market economy we are creating here by
common efforts. How will we restrain domestic fuel
consumption—by the size of the export pipeline? Is there
enough gasoline, or will we have to travel abroad by
bicycle to buy fuel? There is of course a market-economy
answer: Buy it abroad if you do not have enough of your
own. But there is, after all, a prosaic side to life too.

It is expressed in those very same fue! nonpayments:
Borrowed funds make up virtually 100 percent of oil
refineries’ working capital. They do not use a single
kopek of their own money, but they do not pay money to
either their suppliers or the budget. They dispatch gaso-
line somewhere, those people in turn fail to pay for
it—who are the last people in the chain? Nizhniy
Novgorod’s “‘Nefteorgsintez” has set a record: It sent
gasoline worth several billions of rubles over the year to
@ certain joint-stock company, comprising one person.
That person failed to pay, and that was that. Whom can
you love so much apart from your own child that you can
give away 2 billion of someone else’s property?

Meanwhile the defaulter allegedly opens a hard-currency
deposit account for $20 million at 5-percent annual
interest. A record of ingenuousness—not at 100 percent
but a modest 5 percent. While the leadership of another
“‘Nefteorgsintez” immediately opens hard-currency
accounts with the same bank. Specialists here are racking
their brains, shaping concepts for nonpayment, and
arguing abou: manufacturers’ reaction to the complexi-
ties of the transitional period. While the main defaulters
are these joint-stock companies consisting of one to three
people apiece. At the conference in Novosibirsk Chubays
even said that half of all nonpayments are a matter cf
theft.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Thus, the new Gos-
plan [State Planning Committee] is intended to combat
dishonesty among enterprises themselves?

{Livshits] In an even more general form: Dinosaur-
structures that have gotten accustomed to hot, fetid
water are the main force hampering and retarding
reforms. They cannot live on the surface, but they do
have brains and experience. They are therefore recre-
ating the “primordial soup.”” Vague legislation and lack

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

of discipline play into their hands. They are absolutely
apolitical. Everyone who deftly takes their interests into
account will receive a great deal of money for the
elections. Their demagogic slogan is this: Stability in
other words cannot change a thing. “Don’t dare touch
our economic freedom with your dirty hands!”

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] But a return to
administrative levers may cost us Western mistrust of
our reforms. And as a result may mean that we are
denied next year’s line of credit—and that involves over
$13 billion already planned for the budget.

[Livshits} I am relying on the West’s manifesting a depth
of understanding of our problems. We recently went to
the United States and prepared for the president's visit,
for which dozens of meetings were organized. I can say
that all in all the West has a far more optimistic view of
Russia’s prospects than you and I do. The Americans
love to plan everything, including our upturn; they are
making very serious preparations—buying up large
blocks of shares in privatized enterprises and bringing in
large amounts of hard currency, including cash. It is very
doubtful that they vill turn their back on our reforms;
the fact that the limits of liberalization have indeed been
overstepped in a number of areas is another matter.

I will give you just two examples. Prices: We have
already lost natural monopolists—railroad tariffs, for
instance. And then there is privatization. Imagine a large
mine owned by the labor collective. The wonderful
collective has gobbled up what it could, and there is
barely enough money left for current consumption. An
annual meeting of shareholders is convened. And people
say: What about our dividends? Where are our Toyotas
and Sonys? There is no money? How can there be no
money? Well then, we are the masters: We will now get
rid of the director!

What is to be done? In principle you can issue more
shares, thus luring a powerful investor, who will come
alone and tell the workers: Another two years of this
policy and you will not have Toyotas or a mine but just
a fond farewell. Thus I am convinced of the need for
temporary and partial deprivatization. On the principle
of granny’s hope chest—the controlling block of shares
will appear on the market in two or three years, we will
open up the chest and dip into the reserves when the
investors are mature enough. And only in the event of
there being no private investor do we introduce a state
manager. State management is not a one-way street;
there is room here for buying and selling.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Might people be
afraid of investing money in major projects with long
periods of recoupment owing to dramatic dips in the
market like “Black Tuesday”?

[Livshits] As for “Black Tuesday,” there is something of
a red herring in that explanation. For instance, the banks
categorically deny a conspiracy aimed at bringing down
the ruble. Indeed, I do not believe in a conspiracy. It was

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

just a routine case of going into free-fall. i am only
surprised at the shortsightedness shown by the people
involved in the speculation. It would have been OK had
the ruble fallen 100 points, but 900! Political fallout is
inevitable.

Let me draw the following analogy. A man goes to a
clearing and wants to pick all the mushrooms there. He
sees a bear’s lair but does not balk. There are also fine
mushrooms growing there. The bear growls and the man
is amazed: I am only picking mushrooms, I am not
hurting anyone. It is the same with our currency specu-
lators. They simply have no instinct for self-control.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Are you not amazed
that there is none of the customary panic buying at the
end of the year? All the shelves in the stores used to be
completely cleared out, and now even the major traders
have surpluses in their warehouses. Perhaps the people
have finally come to believe in stabilization?

jLivshits] Surpluses on the markets are a healthy sign.
Moreover, a serious and painful process is under way—
the move to a low profit norm, for voth traders and
banks, particularly for the “spoilt children” who
emerged atop the crest of inflation. The Russian
Klondike era is over. The stabilization expected by the
consumer is a fine thing, But something else is frighten-
ing—the change in manufacturers’ expectations. In
August 40 percent of manufacturers had no intention of
raising the prices of their output. But in practice it takes
nine months to change someone’s expectations, like
pregnancy. In another three or four months an irrevers-
ible keenness for stabilization would have emerged. But
“Black Tuesday” ruined everything: Only 19 percent of
directors have no intention of raising prices now. Thus,
the remaining 81 percent are bent on devaluing the
ruble.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] How can you explain
the fact that many Western goods, household and video
goods included, are sold at lower prices in Moscow than
in Europe? Perhaps customs posts on entering Russia
have already been replaced by more effective criminal
cordons which divert imports “onto the black market’’?

[Livshits] There are fairly entrenched, powerful struc-
tures which are exempt from import duties and are
accorded deferments when it comes to paying tax and
excise duties. After all, how does a “dinosaur” reason?
Yesterday after all the exemptions his annual profits
totaled $150 million, say. He sees that 100 million are
left once the concessions are taken away and yells: “We
will not survive! It’s the end of the world!” And on the
quiet seeks other grounds for concessions. Admittedly,
only so long as there is someone to oppose.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Te!! us, is the govern-
ment not preparing on the quiet a gift for its citizens in
the form of more monetary reform?

[{Livshits] I don’t think so. Who can put up a roof
without building a house? Well, we have carried out

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 27

reform, but with this kind of inflation will we to have
start everything from scratch within a year? At some
point it will of course be necessary to carry out a
revaluation of our currency, crossing out the excess zeros
on price tags and banknotes. Or else people will be
carting money around in suitcases. We will exchange
bank notes over a six-month period, say, without secrets
and giving people three days to exchange their notes, and
this will be organized in a deliberately open and unos-
tentatious way. Never mind confiscating any superin-
comes.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Can you predict our
financial situation for next year?

{Livshits] The year 1995 may be a kind of bridge
between the inflationary past and a future of investment.
I am referring to national investors. By the end of the
year, to sum up, we may obtain an annual inflation level
of 50-60 percent and a slightly higher credit rate of 60-70
percent. I like experimenting with directors. I tell a
director: “Your credit rate is 60 percent.” He replies: I
will certainly not come a cropper, I will go to people who
issue money at 60 percent.

There will be a very real rush for spare cash. The federal
government will be jostling for the big bucks—loans for
state bonds. Local authorities will be pushing municipal
loans. Banks and joint-stock enterprises will be doing
their own thing. And then these wheeler-dealers will say:
Give us money for your children’s sake.... It will all be
rainbows, homes, sunshine.... By and large sundry sav-
ings companies which grab you by the throat in an
affectionate manner, with gusto. I can already distin-
guish them by their voices. But the market area is fairly
restricted. And it is quite clear that the government is a
mastodon there, moreover share holders are not
breathing down its neck for dividends. The state debt,
inflation, the budget deficit—everything hinges on how
many state bonds the Central Bank takes on. According
to the government’s plans, inflationary pressure from
that quarter will be less than in 1994.

According to experts’ assessments, 20 percent of private
investors are people knowr ito be risk-takers. He goes
along with an enterprise, but puts his own money in
another company; if he is defrauded there, he goes along
to a third. He is a real glutton for punishment. This
sector of the market only wants offers of get-rich
schemes. But for serious financial companies the time
has come to seriously contemplate the cramped condi-
tions on the money market. Only “shuttle investors” will
remain to provide credit at 200 percent a year and thus
rub along. But those who change their behavior on the
market in favor of more effective productive investment,
reducing the percentage profit, will really survive. How-
ever, they will be looking for money for their projects
under a real government, which will pay them 240
percent a year on its short-term bonds.

[KOMSOMOLSKAYA PRAVDA] Are you not scared
that your rosy forecasts may be easily overturned by the

State Duma’s bold moves like the recent wholesale
raising of the minimum wage?

[Livshits] The raising of the minimum wave from 20,000
to 54,000 [rubles] has been given a first reading by the
State Duma. Then will come the government’s findings
on the draft law, as always happens when it is a matter of
budget expenditure. According to the government, the
rise in the minimum wage translates into enormous
sums: 6.5 trillion rubles a month jusi vy recomputing the
wages of budget-funded personnel and reducing tax
revenue. Moreover, this may bring a rapid increase in
overt unemployment. Directors will start dismissing
people because they will be physically unable to pay their
wages. Things will probably not go so far as a presiden-
tial veto. The Duma will see reason faster than that. But
this is symptomatic. I can already foresee populism in
the 1995 preelection campaign and vying with one
another in expressions of love for ordinary people.

Trade Bill Increases State Regulation

MK1612150094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 16
Dec 94 p 2

[Report by Aleksandr Bekker under the “Government”
rubric: “Etatists Have Pressed Liberals Aside. Foreign
Economic Relations Ministry Tightens State Regula-
tion”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Yet another clash of stances on
the regulation of foreign economic activity took place at
yesterday’s government meeting. The Foreign Economic
Relations Ministry [FERM] presented the draft law “On
Russian Foreign Economic Activity.” According to
Deputy Minister Fradkov, it is “aimed at ensuring the
country’s sovereignty and economic security.” The doc-
ument calls for export and import monopolies to be
introduced on a temporary basis. This provision was
criticized by the liberal wing of the Cabinet of Ministers
for failing to specify how long the non-tariff measures
will be in force. The entire FERM concept was also
criticized for being overly centralized.

Countering the liberals’ criticism, the ministry said it is
ready to give reasons for its course toward tightening the
screws: Russia’s estimated losses from the concealment
and unauthorized transfer of hard-currency earnings
abroad reached $10-12 billion this year. One-hundred
twenty control audits of Russian residents’ compliance
with currency legislation have helped the state reclaim
10 billion rubles, $33 million, and 1 million Deutsche
marks. For this reason the FERM decided to establish
control over the key currency flows and proposed in its
draft law that it be entrusted with the function of
coordinating foreign economic activity.

The draft law has vividly revealed the FERM’s drift
toward an increase in state regulation. This concerns the
retention of the institution of special exporters, and
control over energy resources export via the introduction
of internal quotas. A 100-percent sale of hard-currency

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

earnings by exporters and quotas on the import of
certain products also appear to have been set in motion.

The government’s approval of the draft law means that
the advocates of tighter foreign trade regulation have
gained the upper hand at mid-finish. The Cabinet of
Ministers’s liberal wing under Mr. Chubays still has a
chance, however. First, the draft law was sent back to be
streamlined, taking into account critical remarks;
second, the liberals were below strength at the govern-
ment meeting—the sick Economy Minister Yasin and
his first deputy, Mr. Urinson, were not present. If they
recover by the second hearing, they will try to modify the
draft, unless the sociopolitical situation in the country
changes too abruptly by that time.

Duma Approves Savings Compensation Law

MK1612143094 Moscow SEGODNYA in Russian 16
Dec 94 p 2

[Report by Dmitriy Volkov: “The Duma of Lost Money
[pun, Duma also meaning folk ballad]. The Lower House
Has Adopted a Law ’On Restoring and Protecting Citi-
zens’ Savings”)

[FBIS Translated Text] The state Duma has adopted a
draft law “On Restoring and Protecting Citizens’ Sav-
ings,” and now this document will go to the senate. The
draft establishes state obligations on restoring citizens’
savings lost after price liberalization. Its sponsors—
communist Viktor Zorkaltsev, social democrat Vasiliy
Lipitskiy, and “unprefixed” democrat Sergey Glazyev—
explained that “the task of restoring and protecting the
savings” accumulated by citizens in the “pre-reform
period” is a “necessary precondition for implementing
and enhancing the effectiveness of economic reform.”

The draft law includes the savings lost in the internal
state debt whose redemption the government guarantees
using all the resources at its disposal. The drafters are
counting on the results of the current effort to define the
mechanism of noninflationary servicing and repayment
of the internal debt.

The law proposed to the senate is a framework one, and
all payments will be made in line with other, upcoming,
documents. Therefore the deputies rejected a remark
contained in the government conclusion on the draft.
According to Vasiliy Lipitskiy, the gist of the govern-
ment objections was that when the law enters into force,
the state debt will increase by 400 trillion rubles while
inflation will intensify. Lipitskiy, refuting the opinion of
Cabinet of Ministers experts, said yesterday that the
“debt has already been recognized” under a presidential
edict. Apparently he meant the exchange between Boris
Yeltsin and Rusian Khasbulatov in March 1993, when
the Kremlin and the Supreme Soviet competed in luring
the citizens to their side in the run-up to a referendum on
a in the president. The allusion is not very nice
but fair.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright ownczs.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

One can doubt, however, the fairness of Duma econo-
mists who maintain that the ongoing work on creating
noninflationary mechanisms of covering state expendi-
tures will necessarily lead to the actual emergence of such
mechanisms. State obligations will be recorded in a law,
and if for some reason the mechanism does not work, the
domestic financiers will have either to break the pre-
scriptions of the law and do nothing, or use another,
proven, mechanism: the inflationary one. Interestingly,
the government has estimated a possible failure of these
tests at 400 trillion rubles, which is no mean sum.

It is also noteworthy that the law drafters use the concept
of “purchasing power of the Russian currency,” for
instance, when talking about 1990. Under the law, it is
calculated based on the cost of products, goods, and
services ensuring a “normal physiological existence of
the individual.” This term, however, does not (and
cannot possibly) take into account the physical absence
of products, goods, and services, typical of the “pre-
reform period.” As a result, citizens often saved money
precisely because they could not spend it: Not because of
high prices but because there was nothing to spend the
money on. Of course, the deputies and highly placed
politicians could not have known this at the time, and
this is why they keep silent about it now.

‘Luch’ Communications Satellite Reaches Orbit

LD1612135994 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1348
GMT 16 Dec 94

‘By ITAR-TASS correspondent Viktor Gritsenko]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 16 TASS—
The Russian military space forces today launched a
proton-K booster to put into orbit a Luch retransmiiter
satellite.

The new satellite will control the orbital piloted Mir
station and organise urgent telephone communication in
emergencies.

The booster blasted off from the Baykonur space centre
in Kazakhstan at 15.00 Moscow time (12.00 GMT) on
Friday, December 16, spokesman for the Russian space
forces Ivan Safronov told ITAR-TASS.

Samara Produces Modernized Rocket Motors

LD1812225394 Moscow Mayak Radio Network in
Russian 1450 GMT 18 Dec 94

[FBIS Summary] The Samara Engine Construction Pro-
duction Association has successfully tested a modernized
space engine at its own simulation launch pad.

The development of the modernized engine is part of the
Rus national space program, which involves manned
space missions and other projects at the Plesetsk cosmo-
drome. The modernized engine is more powerful than

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 29

earlier modifications, which means that it can take
heavier cargoes into in orbit.

Designers have managed to adapt existing equipment
and techniques for the assembly of the new engines, thus
saving jobs and cutting production costs.

Anatoliy Annenkov, head of branch at the production
association, predicts that the engine makers should have
enough orders for the next 20-30 years. Serial production
of the engines is planned for 1996.

Official Describes New Space Radar

LD1912115994 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1148
GMT 19 Dec 94

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] December 19. Moscow.
(INTERFAX)—A leading Russian engineering plant,
specializing in military and civilian rockets, has designed
Almaz, a new radar equipped spacecraft that will allow
io probe the earth in any weather.

As Vladimir Viter, first assistant to the plant’s general
designer, has reported to Interfax, Almaz-1B is to be
launched in late 1997 to make it possible to picture the
earth and environment in detail, locate mineral deposits,
monitor the state of ice, oceans and seas, and help in
such emergencies such as fires or accidents. Viter argues
that being short of an Almaz type space probe in orbit,
Russia has been running 60-70 billion rubles in losses
year after year.

A joint-stock Russian-American company (SAR) has
been set up to implement the Almaz-1B project, esti-
mated at $370 million. It comprises the Russian engi-
neering plant (47 percent of the charter capital), the
state-run Rosvocruzhenyie (4 percent), and the U.S.
Sokol Group Inc. (49 percent). Experts say the project
will be repaid in two years after the launch. $120 million
have already been invested.

Duma Passes Law on Presidential Elections

LD1612202494 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1923
GMT 16 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Having considered two alterna-
tives of the draft law on presidential elections introduced
by the president and the parliamentary faction YAB-
LOKO, the State Duma opted for the presidential draft
and passed it in the first hearing. A total of 312 votes
were cast in favor and seven against the document, with
two abstentions.

According to the presidential version of the draft law
developed by the Central Election Commission, only one
presidential candidate may be nominated by either an
election bloc or directly by the electorate. An election
bloc nominates its candidate by secret ballot at its
congress or conference. A presidential candidate can also
be nominated by an initiative group of individuals of at
least 100 persons by canvassing two million signatures in
favor of this candidate. Moreover, no more than seven
percent of signatures can be canvassed in each item of
the federation.

To create equal conditions for all presidential candi-
dates, those employed by a state or municipal body or
mass media will be relieved of their duties for the period
of the election campaign. However, the draft law fails to
provide a mechanism for relieving the acting president
of his duties or restricting them if he joins the race for
another term in the office. Each presidential candidate is
entitled to spend no more than five billion rubles on his
campaign.

A candidate who canvassed over half of the votes is
recognized as president-elect. Elections will be recog-
nized as valid if attended by over half of the electors. If

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

none of the candidates canvass the required number of
votes, a second round of elections is held, provided there
are more than two candidates on the ballot paper. The
president-elect begins his duties on the 30th day from the
official publication of the election returns.

The other version of the draft law proposed by the
faction YABLOKO envisioned canvassing at least
300,000 signatures in favor of a presidential candidate
nominated by both election blocs and individuals’ ini-
tiative groups. The document provides a mechanism for
verifying the authenticity of signatures canvassed. In
addition, the document contains a provision restricting
the possibility of direct or indirect bribing of electors.

Committee Passes No-Confidence Measure on
Shumeyko

LD1612181394 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1617
GMT 16 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow, Dec 16 (INTER-
FAX)—The security and defense committee of Russia’s
Federation Council passed a decision on non-confidence
in the chamber speaker Vladimir Shumeyko because he
failed to inform deputies about the planned introduction
of forces in Chechnya, a member of the committee
Valeriy Fateyev declared in an interview with Interfax
on Friday.

“On December 8 we adopted a resolution on Chechnya
which contains an item on inadmissibility to use coer-
cive methods with respect to the Chechen crisis and on
December 11 I saw that forces eniered Ingushetia,”
Fateyev noted. In his words, “such operations are never
prepared in 24 hours, it is quite clear that on December
8 the decision on forces introduction, which could not
bypass the Security Council, was adopted.”

The Federation Council delegated Shumeyko to the
Security Council and Shumeyko was to inform the
deputies about the operation, Fateyev believes. Thus,
Fateyev said, “the chairman of the chamber either did
not know and did not say, which is very bad, or he did
not know anything, and this is awfully bad.”

In addition, Fateyev said, “the deputies proposed to
discuss these issues, which are fraught with Russia’s
collapse, early this year but the leadership of the Feder-
ation Council has not adopted any appropriate measures
and permitted the present situation which is called a
war.”

Due to this fact, Fateyev said, the security and defense
committee passed a decision “to part with a man who
knew everything but remained silent, who had an oppor-
tunity to consolidate efforts of the deputies but failed to
do this.”

Duma Defense Committee’s Piskunov Cited on

coming year remains unchanged in the future, the reten-
tion of the current number of servicemen would simply
become senseless. Speaking about the Defense Ministry’s
demands (R111 trillion), Aleksandr Piskunov noted that
this figure is unrealistic but at the same time is quite
valid for today’s army.

The deputy warned that only between R1.5 and R2
trillion will be realistically spent on armaments in the
coming year. In Aleksandr Piskunov’s view, the defense
spending sum should be increased by R10.8 trillion; the
additional funds should be channeled in equal portions
for the construction of housing for career military per-
sonnel being discharged into the reserves, and for a
technical rearming of the army. Mr. Piskunov believed
that the closed part of the budget is supposed to show the
following priorities: the retaining by Russia of its nuclear
power status (even if this requires modification of the
START II treaty), and the development of space-based
and high-precision weapons and ammunition. in his
words, the Russian Federation, in its European part
alone, has at its disposal 40,000 tonnes of chemical
weapons awaiting “recycling” which are corroding tank
cars. Also to be destroyed are 80,000 railroad cars with
conventional ammunition, much of which have been
neatly stored since World War I.

Touching on the situation in Chechnya, Aleksandr
Piskunov declared that the army budget will not be able
to bear for long the outlays for the troops there. He also
presumed that if military operations go on, there might
be problems with the spring conscription campaign.

Yegorov Threatens Further Air Strikes

LD1712164994 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1611
GMT 17 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS special correspondent Mikhail
Shevtsov]

{FBIS Transcribed Text] Mozdok, North Ossetia,
December 17 TASS—Russian Vice-Prime Minister
Nikolay Yegorov threatened to launch new air strikes if
Chechen President Dzhokhar Dudayev fails to disarm
his illegal armed groups.

“If Dzhokhar Dudayev fails to state his readiness to
comply with the decision of the Russian leadership on
the disarmament of illegal armed groups by midnight
December 18, 1994, an air strike will be delivered on
strategic facilities in Groznyy in accordance with the
latest decision of the (Russian) Security Council,”
Yegorov told ITAR-TASS upon his return to Mozdok
from Moscow where he had attended a meeting of the
Russian Security Council.

He also stressed that if “he (Dudayev) does not come to
his senses after the strike, the bands will be destroyed.”

Stepashin has arrived in Mozdok along with Yegorov.
The ministers of the interior and defence, Viktor Yerin
and Pavel Grachev, remain in Moscow. Stepashin
refused to comment on the causes of the delay in their
arrival.

Earlier today, Yegorov issued a similar warning saying
that “if adequate measures for disarming illegal armed
groups on the territory of the Chechen Republic are not
taken before midnight December 18, a missile strike will
be delivered on strategic facilities in Groznyy.”

Russian Security Council Secretary Oleg Lobov also
stressed today that the deadline set by President Boris
Yeltsin for illegal armed bands to lay down their arms
will not be extended. He pointed out that if Dudayev
fails to meet the deadline, Russian Interior Ministry
troops and the army may use force to disarm armed
groups.

Dudayev said on Friday he was ready to resume talks on
any terms, but demanded that Russian troops be with-
drawn from Chechnya first.

However, on Saturday he refused to arrive in Mozdok
for talks with the Russian delegation led by Yegorov And
stepashin. He also invited Russian Prime Minister
Viktor Chernomyrdin to come to Groznyy for talks on
Chechen settlement.

“I welcome Viktor Chernomyrdin’s willingness to con-
duct talks with us and, as chairman of the Cabinet of
Ministers of the Chechen Republic, I am ready to receive

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 33

him in Groznyy at any time,”” Dudayev said at a briefing
after meeting his military leaders on Saturday.

He emphasised specifically that “if the meeting takes
place, it should be held on a government level.”

Lobov who chaired the council session on Saturday
pointed out that Russia is prepared to talk with the
Chechen leader only aboui the disarmament of illegal
armed groups in Chechnya.

“Only a voluntary surrender of arms by all illegal armed
groups on the territory of the Chechen Republic can be
the subject of talks with Dzhokhar Dudayev.” “If this
does not happen, no other questions will be discussed,”
he added.

Lobov advised civilians to leave Groznyy, adding that it
was impossible to ensure their security. He noted that
those who wish to leave the town should use security
corridors in the southern part of the city which has
already been blockaded from north, east and west.

ment to arrive for talks in Mozdok with a Russian

Nikolay Yegorov and FSK [Federal Counter-Intelligence
Service] director Sergey Stepashin. When journalists put

Oleg Lobov said he did not “know anything about that.”

tions in Chechnya to voluntarily surrender arms and the
amnesty deadline established in the address of the Rus-
sian President “would not be extended.” Thus Dudayev
had a matter of hours left to take the decision to disarm
all the illegal groups operating in Chechnya, he stressed.
Following the expiry of the deadline Defense and Inte-
rior Ministry troops could begin a disarmament opera-
tion with the use of force, Oleg Lobov added.

The secretary of ihe Security Council urgently advised
the civilian population of Groznyy to leave the town as it
seemed impossible to guarantee the safety of civilians
and journalists now in Groznyy. To this end he advised
them to use the so-called safety corridors through which

Groznyy Reported Calm as Deadline Passes

LD1712225894 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 2222 GMT 17 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Sharip Asuyev]

[FBIS Translated Text] Groznyy, 18 Dec—In the first
few minutes since the deadline for the disarmament of
illegal armed formations in Chechnya has expired, a
combat aircraft flew over Groznyy at high altitude.
Apart from this, the situation in the town seems calm.

The streets are being patrolled by home guard members
who are well armed. The town’s services are working as
usual. There have been no disruptions of electricity,
water, or gas supplies. Local television is still broad-
casting. It is showing reports from regions of confronta-
tion between the Chechen defense forces and subunits of
Russia’s Interior and Defense ministries. According to
Dudayev’s headquarters, there is a lull in all sectors.

People who stayed in the town have left their lights on.
They probably hope there will be no military actions.

Dudayev Telephone Conversation Intercepted

LD1812143494 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Service in
Russian 1440 GMT 17 Dec 94

[FBIS Translated Text] Moscow, 17 Dec—The press
service of the government of the Russian Federation
circulated the following report today:

Implementing the decree No. 2166 by the president of
the Russian Federation of 9 December 1994 “On mea-
sures to stop the activity of illegal armed formations” on
the territory of the Chechen Republic, specialists have
intercepted a telephone conversation that took place on
the Nalchik-Groznyy line. The purpose of the conversa-
tion was to coordinate unlawful actions and to draw into
the conflict citizens of Kabarda-Balkaria and other
North Caucasian republics in order to give a regional
nature to the conflict. In the circumstances the recording
of the conversation was passed on to competent bodies
to identify the interlocutor. It has been established that
the conversation took place between Sufiyan Beppayev,
a citizen of the Republic of Kabarda-Balkaria, and
Dzhokhar Dudayev.

We offer to the attention of the media the content of the
conversation between two retired generals without any
comments so that the media can reach their own conclu-
sion:

Beppayev: Dear Dzhokhar, how are things?
Dudayev: A wide front has already been opened.

Beppayev: What do you mean “opened’’? Everyone is in
Nazran. I’ve spoken to Zalimkhan.

Dudayev: They will be most aggressive from the direc-
tion of Nadterechnyy rayon. They have been blocked in
the area of Dagestan.

Beppayev: We have been told that, their chairman of
parliament has been here. Even they were indignant: it
should be done on the site, they gave a guarantee that not
a single item of equipment will filter through from
Dagestan.

Dudayev: And it has filtered through and approached
right up Gerdzel. And all this equipment came through
Dagestan.

Beppayev: Bastards.

Dudayev: Bastards. And there has not been a single word
from the Ingush leaders. The people there are rising, the
Ingush and Dagestan people are rising but the leaders are
sitting idle and keeping quiet.

Beppayev: Cowards.

Dudayev: The mean cowards. How did it go in the
Confederation?

this carnage.

pelled to do it.

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 17 TASS—
Russian President Boris Yeltsin signed a decree on

Shumeyko’s Peace Initiative Assessed

MK1712110094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 17 Dec 94 p 3

[Report by Nika Stark: ““Vladimir Shumeyko’s Initiative.
Two Speakers and Premier Will Continue President’s
Cause”’}

{[FBIS Translated Text] The development of the
“Chechen situation” in Russian upper echelons is
moving forward quickly. Hardly haa presidential
address No. 2 to the Chechen people appeared, Vladimir
Shumeyko came forward with a new peacemaking initia-
tive the next morning. The proposal to create a “trium-
virate on Chechnya’’ (Chernomyrdin-Rybkin-
Shumeyko) cannot but spawn various versions especially
at a time when the president continues to stay in the
hospital while the conference of Security Council mem-
bers is being chaired by the premier.

According to an official version supported by Vladimir
Shumeyko himself, Shumeyko’s initiative on calling a
cease-fire beginning at 1500 on 16 December and setting
up a Supervisory-Conciliatory Commission on
Chechnya with wide-ranging control-administrative

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

functions and jointly chaired by the premier and the two
speakers is nothing short of a “deepening” of Boris
Yeltsin’s address made the day before yesterday. This
version appears quite plausible, although it does raise
some questions.

In accordance with the widely used practices among the
Russian top leadership, only the president has the right
to such initiatives. Up to now, he has never shared this
right with anyone. Of course, a voluntary transfer of the
laurels of solver of the Chechen problem to Shumeyko
can be attributed to the president’s ailment (the officially
named eight-day term required for the recovery of
Yeltsin’s nasal septum expired only yesterday). Never-
theless, one more version appears quite plausible about
the peacemaking initiative’s being not transferred, but
grabbed.

The possible authors of the “grabbing” are named in the
announcement itself. At least two of them are recognized
aspirants for the presidency, and it is a long wait until
1996. Strangely, yet another figure rather influential
both on the federal and regional levels was not included
in this list. The reason, possibly, is that Yuriy Luzhkov is
not among Shumeyko’s friends. Besides, whereas the
creation of the “troika” is a game, playing it was greatly
facilitated by the mayor’s absence from Moscow—he
was abroad throughout the recent Chechen develop-
ments.

It is hard to judge how spontaneous and local Shum-
eyko’s initiatives are. The instant response by Dzhokhar
Dudayev who, without any official talks, announced his
orders to the Chechen detachments to cease fire, can
indicate either that one of the three (or one of the
“power” ministers tasked by them) contacted the
Chechen leader in advance, or the latter’s exceptionally
correct political reaction.

And if the “troika” manages to solve the Chechen
problem in the same way as they made do without the
president in the evening conference of the Security
Council, elections may occur sooner not only in
Chechnya but also in Russia.

‘Strange’ Security Council Session Viewed

MK1712125094 Moscow KOMMERSANT-DAILY in
Russian 17 Dec 94 p 3

[Report by Ilya Buiavinov: “Ministers Leave Mozdok To
Meet in Moscow”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Around 1600 yesterday, news
agencies reported that Nikolay Yegorov, Pavel Grachev,
Viktor Yerin, and Sergey Stepashin left Mozdok for
Moscow to attend a meeting of the Security Council. The
meeting was expected to sum up the results of the
one-week operation “to restore constitutional order” in
Chechnya.

Boris Yeltsin’s health appears to have changed drasti-
cally during the day. As early as yesterday morning it

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 37

enabled the president to receive U.S. Vice President
Albert Gore, but in the evening Boris Yeltsin was already
unable to attend the planned Security Council meeting.
As a result, the most inconceivable rumors spread
around Moscow, while official sources reported that it
was a meeting “of a number of Security Council mem-
bers” under Viktor Chernomyrdin.

It should be noted that previously not a single Security
Council meeting was held without the president.
Another strange thing is that the meeting was chaired not
by Council Secretary Oleg Lobov (which would have
been only natural in the president’s absence), but the
prime minister. Apparently the answer is that Lobov and
other Security Council specialists opposed large-scale
military operations in Chechnya. As for yesterday’s
meeting, it is clear that “the post-flight critique session”
could well have had fully negative consequences for the
power ministers and the counterintelligence chief
(rumors have it that his position is most unenviabie),
unless someone had deliberately planned to drag out the
military operation, of course.

At the same time, the Security Council’s proposals are
not binding on the president. He alone is entitled to
make final decisions, which are subsequently given the
form of edicts and directives. If Boris Yeltsin has his own
plans with regard to Chechnya which are at odds with the
Security Council’s proposals, yesterday’s meeting may
well not affect Moscow’s Chechen policy in any way.
Something of this kind has already taken place.

Another option is also possible. If the decision made by
yesterday’s meeting is nonetheless put into life, this
would mean that Viktor Chernomyrdin’s clout has seri-
ously increased as a result of the Chechen crisis. But, by
the same token, the prime minister would share the
responsibility that was previously shouldered by Boris
Yeltsin alone.

It is also possible that the prime minister and the chiefs
of the Federal Assembly chambers may have used the
occasion to discuss Vladimir Shumeyko’s initiative,
which calls for a triumvirate to be formed to resolve all
problems concerning Chechnya. Since the range of such
problems could be fairly wide, the triumvirate’s clout
could also be quite significant. [Article includes the
following boxed commentary by the Political Depart-
ment]

A resumption of large-scale combat operations by the
federal troops after the ultimatum expires 18 December
looks problematic. Until now the military has not
destroyed any “Grad” multiple-rocket launcher or elim-
inated any significant number of the enemy's armored
hardware, although the sites where the rocket launchers
are deployed should have been located and the launchers
disabled before the operation started. The ABC’s of
tactics required that the “‘iilegal armed formations”
should have been localized outside Groznyy, but now
they can at any moment attack the Russian forces from

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

38 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

the rear, something that might well be a major turning
point in the current situation.

In these conditions, the most effective operation for the
special services would be a “neutralization” of Dzhokhar
Dudayev, a symbol of anti-Russian sentiments in Chech-
nya. By some accounts, the military is leaning precisely
toward this option, in the belief that this would help to
ease tension in the region and to regain the strategic
initiative.

Stepashin—in Mozdok on Sunday.

In his telegram sent to Dudayev, Yeltsin stressed that “if
an appropriate document on a cease-fire is signed on

it as proof of your serious intention to normalise the
situation, to restore peace and calm in the republic for
the sake of all peoples of Russia.”

Dudayev responded by saying that he was ready to hold
talks with Yeltsin’s representatives in Groznyy, without
saying whether or not he would meet his envoys in
Mozdok.

But speaking at a news conference in Groznyy several
hours later, the rebel Chechen leader rejected Yeltsin's

or Prime Minister Viktor Chernomyrdin.

oughly prepared.”

be no more responses to Dzhokhar Dudayev’s tele-
grams.”

Boris Yeltsin, in which he said that he was “ready to
receive (in Groznyy) a plenipotentiary representative” of
the Russian president for talks, was not a proper
response to President Yeltsin’s peace offer.

passenger and cargo trains had been examined. Outside
Chechnya, the Interior Ministry and Federal Counterin-
telligence Service are exercising control over the leaders
of the Chechen criminal groupings.

Caucasian Confederation Set To Aid Chechnya
LD1912120294 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1145
GMT 19 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow. Dec 19. (Interfax)—
The Deputy Chairman of the parliament of the Confed-
eration of Caucasian Peoples (CCP) Denga Khalidov has

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 41

confirmed that the CCP decided to set up headquarters
“for giving comprehensive aid to Chechnya and for
organizing the spontaneous movement of volunteers.”

As he explained to Interfax, such headquarters were set
up in localities “where the CCP has real influence - from
Dagestan to Abkhazia.”

According to Khalidov, such headquarters were set up in
Makhachkala and Khasav-Yurt (Dagestan), Nalchik
(Kabardino-Balkaria), Maikop (Adigeya), Sukhumi
(Abkhazia), Nazran (Ingushetia), Vladikavkaz (North
Ossetia) and Cherkassk (Karachayevo-Cherkessia).

As Khalidov said, the directive to set up headquarters
may have been signed by the CCP chief of staff Amin
Zekhov.

The chairman of the CCP parliament Ali Aliev did not
sign the document (Interfax’s note: on setting up head-
quarters), but he signed the resolution on setting up such
headouarters, issued by the recent CCP congress, Kha-
lidov said.

According to him, “more than one thousand people”
have been despatched to Chechnya through CCP head-
quarters.

“We shall continue to be guided by the congress’s
resolutions and help the Chechen Republic in every way
possible, in organizing the movement of volunteers too,”
Khalidov said.

With reference to Saturday’s resignation of the CCP
President Musa Shanibov Khalidov made it clear that
his stand began to differ from the attitude of “most CCP
leaders.”

“In connection with the events developing in Chechnya
he evidently finds it hard to take decisions that require
resolve, consistency and the like,” Khalidov said. He
pointed out, however, that it is his personal opinion,
since he had not discussed this matter with Shanibov
himself.

“Shanibov did a lot of good at the time when the CCP
was establishing itself, particularly during developments
in Abkhazia,” Khalidov said. “He has done enough,
considering his age.”

In the meantime, judging by what Shanibov himself said,
he resigned because of his health condition, which dete-
riorated as a result of a wound he received in Abkhazia
last year. Moreover, Shanibov said, in the past few days
CCP activists “again raised the issue of urgently giving
Chechnya radical aid,” and “‘new strength is needed” to
resolve such issues.

The CCP presidium decided that the emergency congress
of Caucasian peoples will resume its work on Saturday,
Khalidov said.

Migration Service Coping With Refugee Influx

LD1912131494 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1228
GMT 19 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 19 ITAR-
TASS—Russian migration services in regions neigh-
bouring on the Chechen Republic have registered 12,577
people as refugees from the Chechen Republic since
December 8, 1994, according to the press secretary of the
Federal Migration Service.

In Dagestan, the number of refugees from Chechnya has
exceeded 8.5 thousand. All of them have been provided
with temporary lodgings.

New tents with warm lining, blankets, bed linen and
warm clothes have been prepared for the refugees, and
the number of people in need of humanitarian aid is
being ascertained, according to the heads of Dagestan’s
local administrations.

The Federal Migration Service press centre added that a
special operation group has been set up in the city of
Khasav-Yurt to provide accommodation for refugees
and help those willing to reach Russia’s seven regions
which are ready to receive forced migrants from Chech-
nya.

The overwhelming number of migrants from the
Chechen Republic, however, are reluctant to travel far
and intend to bide their time in areas adjacent on their
native land.

Kabardino-Balkaria is now providing refuge to 160
people from the armed conflict zone.

Some 600 people have turned for help to migration
services located in the Stavropol territory. Some of them
have been accommodated in the areas set apart for
temporary residence, the rest have been expedited to
other regions of Russia.

In the Ingush Republic, a Nazran migration service
official, Aina Barakoyeva told ITAR-TASS that the
service registers up to 80 migrating families a day, the
most its tiny staff can cope with. Tens of people are
awaiting their turn in a long queue. By 11.00 on Monday
the service had registered 856 families or 4,988 persons.

The Federal Migration Service of Russia has undertaken
to render material assistance to, and help resettle people
fieeing from Chechnya in centres and temporary resi-
dence areas across Russia.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

The federal service has provided humanitarian aid to
people in the areas adjacent on Chechnya, including food
parcels with cereals and meat preserves. Each of three
districts in the Ingush republic has got 500 kilograms of
preserves and 800 kilograms of cereals.

Federal migration service chief Tatiana Regent told
itar-tass today that many residents of Chechnya are
reluctant to travel far for fear of being barred from
returning home. She assured that “not a single person
will be transported out of chechnya against his or her
will” and that “following the normalisation of the situ-
ation in Chechnya, the federal service will work to create
conditions for the return all of those willing to their
former place of residence”’.

This statement came in response to the official statement
circulated by Chechen television with reference to
Dzhokhar Dudayev which alleged that Russia was inter-
ested in making civilians leave the Chechen Republic in
order to be further assimilated in Russia. The official
Groznyy said “today’s migration is akin to the 1944
deportation, and none of those who now leaves the
republic will be allowed to return”.

Ilyushenko Interviewed on Chechnya
LD1812003394

[FBIS Editorial Report] Moscow Russian Television
Network in Russian at 2035 on 17 December broadcasts
an 18-minute recorded program entitled “Chechnya: A
Legal Vacuum.” The program consists of an interview
with acting Russian Procurator General Aleksey Ilyush-
enko by Andrey Pishchayev.

Ilyushenko recalls that the then Russian parliament
ruled the presidential and Supreme Soviet elections in
Chechnya in 1991 illegal and, for three years now, the
Constitution and other laws of the Russian Federation
have not been observed in Chechnya. Ilyushenko adds:
“The question is, how to achieve a situation where the
Russian Constitution and Russian laws are observed in
Chechnya. The law does not envisage any mechanism for
this, and here, I believe, both the Federation Council and
the State Duma have to bring their influence to bear now
that the President has done the same. On 8 December
the Federation Council adopted a decision instructing
the Russian Procurator General’s office to check and
assess the actions of officials who had been, I quote,
party to the events in November and December 1994 in
the Chechen Republic. Unfortunately, here only the
second part of the problem is addressed. We cannot
assess these actions because first, one has to sort out
what has been happening in the Chechen Republic for
the past three years, and not just in November and
December 1994, when the Federation Council suddenly
addressed the issue and started dealing with it.”

Ilyushenko goes on to say that many officials, including
Sergey Shakhray, had made applications to the Procu-
rator General’s office with regard to crime in Chechnya.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

He adds: “Unfortunately, we have no means to take any
measures because for the procuracy to start taking mea-
sures and giving assessments it has, for a start, to get to
the Chechen Republic. Only when we can discover on
the spot what is going on there, only then can we give an
assessment. But in these years we had no chance to do so.
At present, in terms of the Russian Constitution and law,
there is no procuracy, no Interior Ministry in the sense
we understand it, no Federal Counterintelligence Service
there, and so on. In practical terms, we just cannot use
any legal levers and means to restore order there.”

Ilyushenko then confirms that some Russian commercial
banks granted Chechens big loans, which have never
been returned. He says there have been other underhand
financial deals, and counterfeiting is thriving in Chech-
nya. In 1993 nearly ten billion rubles worth of counter-
feit banknotes were confiscated in Russia, of which four
billion were linked to Chechens. In 1994, one in three
persons prosecuted for counterfeiting was a Chechen. He
adds: “I can say that Chechnya is one of the main
hotbeds of criminal mafia in this country. According to
the data from the Main Directorate for Criminal Inves-
tigations of the Interior Ministry, at present over 1,200
Chechens in Chechnya are wanted in connection with
taking hostages, committing murders, extortion, banking
fraud, and other serious crimes.” He goes on to say that
64 Chechens were wanted for committing serious crimes
in Moscow and Moscow Oblast, 15 in St. Petersburg and
St. Petersburg Oblast, 51 in Dagestan, 87 in Stavropol
Kray, 32 in Rostov Oblast, and 13 in North Ossetia.
Many criminals are finding refuge in Chechnya after
committing murders and other crimes in neighbouring
regions. He adds: “The fact is that the present situation
in Chechnya is undoubtedly affecting the entire south of
Russia in terms of crime. In Moscow alone, we and the
Interior Ministry have now instituted criminal proceed-
ings in 1,292 cases involving Chechens, with 116 people
facing criminal charges. In St. Petersburg the figures are
similar.” Ilyushenko goes on to talk about Chechens’
involvement in several cases of hostage taking in
southern Russia 1991-94, saying: “In some of these cases
we have testimonies from criminals admitting they
received direct orders from Chechen special services.”
Ilyushenko then gives figures for attacks on goods and
passenger trains, presumably in Chechnya, 2nd says that
it has finally been decided to use other routes bypassing
Chechnya.

Asked if any criminal proceedings will be instituted
following claims by Federation Council deputy Viktor
Kurochkin about bomb strikes on civilian targets in
Chechnya, Ilyushenko says: “I take full responsibility for
saying that as soon as normal order is restored in
Chechnya, the necessary number of investigators and
procurators will be sent. But in order to find out how and
on what targets the bombings were conducted one has to
be there. Until order is restored I cannot send my people,
investigators, and procurators because, unlike the Fed-
eration Council deputy, they do not enjoy deputy’s

RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS 43

immunity. One should not interpret this as the procu-
racy’s refusal to carry out its functions—but we ask all
the powers that be—the president, the government, the
Federal Assembly—to restore order there. Only after
that can they demand that the procuracy, the Interior
Ministry and the Federal Counterintelligence Service
bring to book all those guilty, including those guilty of
any illegal bombings that there may have been. But I do
not know, and you do not know, and nobody can say
anything objectively at this stage, I assure you.”

Ilyushenko goes on to say that criminal proceedings have
been instituted in two cases connected with the advance-

Ingushetia, saying: ““You know that there was a conflict
in Dagestan, in which some servicemen were taken
hostage and later released. In the Ingush Republic, when
the troops were advancing there, armed people were
firing on them, using women and children asa shield. We
have instituted criminal proceedings following our
inquiry, and an investigation is now under way. As you
know, the president of Ingushetia claimed that the troops
started firing on civilians without provocation. As it
happens, the troops were indeed advancing along a road
in accordance with their orders, without disturbing the
peace of the population, but their way was blocked by
concrete slabs, buses, cars, people, and so on. And they
were fired upon point-blank from behind these obstacles.
As a result, the troops returned fire on several occasions.
Some people were wounded and killed on both sides. I
mean the people who were preventing the troops from
moving forward, but several servicemen were also seri-
ously wounded.

“Another example I would like to adduce: When some
vehicles were stopped, they included a military medical
column. The crowd that stopped them plundered the
vehicles, overturning them and so on. They also took
three women medics out of the vehicles and beat them
up te such an extent that they are all currently in
intensive care, one with a broken spine.”

Ilyushenko then refutes reports that the Confederation of
the Peoples of the Caucasus has resolved to send volun-
teers to Chechnya. He says the reports were based on a
forgery, adding: ““The procuracy has already completed
its inquiry and instituted criminal proceedings. We
started the inquiry as soon as the report was shown on
television. What happened was this: INTERFAX faxed a
document, which purported to say that in accordance
with a decision taken at a congress of the Confederation
of the Peoples of the Caucasus, headquarters were being
set up in several towns, including Rostov, Krasnodar,
Stavropol, Lazarevskoye near Sochi, etc., 15 in all. But
when we started to check the fax it transpired that the fax
was made on the notepaper of the president of the
Confederation of the Peoples of the Caucasus. It should
have had—I stress that it had none—a signature of the
chairman of parliament. But there was no such signature.
A bit lower, below the text, it said correct, some name
was given and there was a stamp on top of the name—a

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

44 RUSSIA NATIONAL AFFAIRS

stamp from the Congress of the Kabarda People. So the
forgery was obvious. It was very easy to have a look at all
the inconsistencies and understand that it was not a
document to be trusted. But we went further and I
instructed the procurator of Kabarda-Balkarian

Ingush President Calls for Troop Withdrawal

MM1612211594 Moscow SOVETSKAYA ROSSIYA in
Russian 17 Dec 94 p 1

[Interview with Republic of Ingushetia President Ruslan
Aushev by Nadezhda Garifullina, datelined “yesterday,
during a recess of the Federation Council session”: ““‘It Is
Impossible To Subdue a People””}

{[FBIS Translated Text] Yesterday the Federation
Council discussed the issue of the situation in Chechnya
in closed session. In the recess I asked Ingush Republic
President Ruslan Aushev to answer a few questions.

[Garifullina] Ruslan Sultanovich, as far as is known from
press reports, you are the only leader of a North Cauca-
sian republic not to sign the Appeal to the Russian
president on the imposition of order in Chechnya, using
every means to do so, and you have a special stance on
this issue. Also well known are your words to the effect
that the Chechens and the Ingush are one people—the
Veinakh—and the protest of the citizens of your republic
over the strong-arm solution of the Chechen problem is
natural.

[Aushev} First, even if the Chechen people did not enter
the equation, and such a decision had been adopted
against, for example, the Kabardino-Balkar, the Dages-
tan, or any other people, I would have acted in the same
way. Why do all quarrels which arise, including over a
difference of views or positions (for example, Groznyy
sees development from one angle, Moscow from
another) have without fail to be resolved by coercive
methods, and not by political means? That is to say, it is
necessary to sit down at the negotiating table and monot-
onously, at length, stubbornly, and painstakingly, using
conciliation commissions and procedures of some
kind—as is written in the Constitution—resolve the
problems and untangle the knots.

Yes, heads of republic appealed to the president, but I
think—I am sure—that many did not actually perfectly
understand /what/ [published in boldface] they were

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

signing, and are now complaining about what has been
done. The strong-arm solution of this problem was
virtually “‘okayed.”

[Garifullina] You evidently possess reliable information.
How do you assess the situation? What is happening in
Chechnya right now?

[Aushev] War, war of the most open kind. We, at least,
know this. First, troops marched through Ingushetia, not
standing on ceremony. And today, when I asked a
candidate for the post of constitutional judge whether
sending in troops is constitutional, he simply did not
reply. The only thing that he could say was: “I am for a
peaceful resolution of the problem.” But if troops are
sent through Ingushetia, a federation component, I, as
president, have to explain to citizens what these troops
are. I ask what their task is, and again I receive no reply.
The troops who marched through Ingushetia used
weapons in violation of all rules and norms. We had five
people killed and 22 wounded, and homes were
destroyed.

Having been sent into the Chechen Republic the Army
has since 13 December been carrying out full-blooded
military operations. There have been very many casual-
ties on both sides. People are being killed and equipment
is being destroyed. Virtually the entire people in
Chechnya have risen up. They talk of disarming illegal
armed formations, but I cannot imagine how they will
now disarm the whole people. From 15-year-old boys—
and even younger—everyone who can hold a gun,
including women, have risen to the defense of their
republic. And I know the mood of the people—they are
ready to stand fast to the end. Volunteers are arriving
from all the republics, the Chechen Republic enjoys the
moral support of virtually everyone. | was informed by
my vice president that a rally is being held in Ingushetia
at which around 50,000 people gathered; the Ingush are
appealing to the authorities to stop the bloodshed.

I know for a fact that soldiers in the Russian forces are
going there reluctantly, that they are ready to turn back
and leave today, if the political will is shown and
political decisions made.

[Garifullina] What do you see as the way out of the
current situation now that the conflict has gone so far
and so much blood has been spilt?

[Aushev] The bloodshed must be stopped, the troops
must be withdrawn, and this should not be considered a
defeat or a humiliation. Of course, it is clear that the
Army will reach Groznyy, that military might there will
be constantly increased; the country is large and its
might is great. But who knows even one example in
history whereby it was possible to destroy a people? In
the name of what is blood being spilled? After all, come
what may, it will be necessary to sit down at the
negotiating table. And in this situation any leader who
himself comes in or is sent in will, whatever happens, be

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

in the eyes of the population an enemy of his own people,
and no one will recognize him! Therefore—let us have
only talks, high-level talks.

[Garifullina} Have you been in touch with Dudayev?
What is his position?

[Aushev] Yes, we have been in touch. His inclination
before the troops were sent in was to hold talks; he even
tried to do this, and went to meet with Grachev. He
sensed that troops were going to be sent in and many
people would be killed. Even today he speaks about this
[talks]. But the troops must be withdrawn.

[Garifullina} How do you assess Ruslan Khasbulatov’s
peacekeeping mission?

{Aushev] His peacekeeping mission? You see where it
has led. It is hard for me to explain what his role is, but
all his slogans about coming as a peacemaker have led to
bloodshed.

[Garifullina] Some people claim that the Russian Army
supports the Chechen opposition. Is this true?

{[Aushev] Of course it has been supporting it! Who do
you think gave it weapons, equipment, mercenaries, and
money? All this was official, and now it has become
known that this was indeed one of the options for solving
the Chechen problem—through arming the opposition.

Dagestan Calls For Peaceful Solution

LD1612221494 Moscow Russian Television Network in
Russian 2000 GMT 16 Dec 94

[Report by M. Tumanova; from the “Vesti” newscast]

{[FBIS Translated Text] At the Dagestan permanent
mission in Moscow today, journalists were given an
explanation of the official position on Chechnya, Dages-
tan’s next-door neighbor. Year after year Dagestan was
suffering from conflicts on the other side of the little-
enforced border. People there have already got used to
communications that have been destroyed, disrupted
supplies of the most vital things, and agitation among the
Akkints Chechens who live on Dagestan territory.

At present, the Akkints have organized a continuous
rally near the settlement of Khasav-Yurt at which they
are demanding an end to the hostilities in Chechnya.
Volunteers among the Akkints Chechens are being called
up from the villages.

Relations between the Dagestan authorities and the
Dudayev regime have always been very cold, but now
everyone in Dagestan demands that the Chechen issue be
resolved peacefully.

Dagestan minister for nationalities Magomed-Salikh
Gusayev said it was impossible to seize Groznyy without
many thousands of victims, and that it is not clear at all
who will govern Chechnya if Dudayev is toppled, and
that the conflict, if Groznyy falls, will rekindle anew. The
best way is the way of exhausting talks. In this connec-
tion Gusayev supported today’s statement by Vladimir
Shumeyko.

Fight today and peace tomorrow is not the way things are
done in the Caucasus, the minister said. Here, if there is
a war, it is for a long time.

Cossacks Urge ‘Full Force,’ Offer To Help
MM1612205794 Moscow TRUD in Russian 17 Dec 94
pl

[Report by Vasiliy Natykin: “Cossacks Sound the
Alarm”]

[FBIS Translated Text] Mozdok—The inhabitants of the
Caucasus Cossacks’ communities are losing their last
shreds of patience. Armed formations, often of unknown
allegiance, are prospering on plundering. killings, and
violence. The Cossacks spoke of the complete lawless-
ness which is prevailing not only in Chechnya, but also in
other regions of the North Caucasus. Your blood runs
cold on hearing these stories. Thus Chiplygin, a 60-year
old inhabitant of the village of Chernokozovo, and his
48-year old fellow villager Shipitsyn were guarding their
grain. In the night, several young guys drove up. Ship-
itsyn came out at the sound of the engine, and was shot
at point-blank range from an assault rifle. The same fate
befell his comrade, who had managed to fire a shot in
self-defense.

In the morning the villagers discovered the bodies of
their fellow villagers. They had been brutally defiled.
Their throats were slit, their stomachs cut open, and
their hearts had been torn out. It was horrifying to look
at the photographs which the atamans were showing.

Pavel Zadorozhnyy, field ataman of the Cossack Union,
stated that they had come to Mozdok to meet with
Russian Vice Premier Nikolay Yegorov and assure him
that all of the Cossacks and inhabitants of settlements

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

and villages in the North Caucasus support the govern-
ment and the president on the issues connected with
restoring law and order.

“I do not understand,” Zadorozhnyy said, “how many
more dead, raped, or missing people we need before
constitutional order is resolutely restored.”

Viktor Zorin, chief of the military administration of the
Caucasus Cossacks, added that 169 officers and generals
are prepared to use their skill and knowledge to help
“untie the Chechen knot of contradictions.”

The Cossacks reported that a rally of representatives of
75 communities has been held in Pyatigorsk. The reso-
lution adopted at the rally was signed, apart from by the
Cossacks, by the Ossetian, Armenian, and Greek com-
munities, the Unior of Officers, and the Union of
Veterans.

The Council of Aiamans of the Russian Cossack Union
has sent a statement to Russian President Boris Yeltsin
and Vice Premier Nikolay Yegorov. They are notably
demanding “that full force be applied” against
Dudayev’s formations and that the Cossacks be brought
in to back up this operation. They are also asking that
three battalions be formed from the Terek Cossack
Troops in cooperation with the Russian organs of
internal affairs throughout the territory of the North
Caucasus to ensure constitutional order and the protec-
tion of national economic installations, and to curb the
activities of the bandit formations.

At the meeting with the Cossacks, Vice Premier Nikolay
Yegorov promised to examine this statement.

‘Experts’ Claim Army ‘Dragged Into’ Conflict
MK1712092094 Moscow NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA in
Russian 17 Dec 94 p 2

[Report by Nikolay Vishnevskiy under the “Army”
rubric with the general heading: ““Groznyy Offered Dia-
logue”)

[FBIS Translated Text] According to reports from very
high-ranking military circles, the situation coming about
in the area of the Chechen conflict is not as cloudless as
is being presented by the authorities.

Yesterday, the sources said that what is discernible
behind the current actions of the Russian Army is the
implementation of political decisions, but not clear-cut
plans of combat operations.

The experts think that the Defense Ministry and the
General Staff did not plan an operation on a settlement
of the Chechen crisis by force, but were dragged into an
internal political conflict under pressure from the top
political leadership. They confirmed that the Armed
Forces plans, hastily drawn up at the directive from the
top, include the sealing off of Chechnya and its capital,
support for the Internal Affairs Ministry units’ effort to
disarm “illegal armed formations,” and the prevention

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

of similar formations from penetrating the republic’s
territory. According to NEZAVISIMAYA GAZETA’s
sources, the plans apparently did not feature an assault
on Groznyy.

It was also confirmed that the “assault” on the city 26
November was “manifestly amateurish in nature,” did
not have a direct bearing on the Army, and appears to
have been carried out by different structures.

The consequences of a military solution to a political
conflict in Chechnya, the experts think, have not been
thought out or assessed whatsoever. In particular, no
provisions have been made for possible casualties among
both servicemen and civilians; economic costs, the
morale and psychological state of the troops and the
Russian population in general were not taken into
account. Similarly, the ill-conceived, inconsistent, and
hasty actions give grounds to suggest that the political
results of turning the North Caucasus region into a zone
of constant instability and of significantly reducing the
state’s defense capability on its southern boundaries
were not considered either.

The experts also see Boris Yeltsin’s latest statement
extending the deadline for compliance with the presiden-
tial edict as confirming the lack of thought-out plans of
action.

In the view of some high-ranking officers in Moscow and
on the ground, this delay may equally bring about both a
political settlement and an assault on Groznyy. It is not
ruled out that by that time the requisite force will be
concentrated around the Chechen capital, reserves will
be pulled up, and—in case talks shculd fail—a decision
will be made to take the city by force.

In the experts’ judgment, any outcome of the conflict will
backfire on the army, which turned out to be “set up”
and which will find itself in a fix that will be even worse
than what it is in now.
