UN Team’s Findings on Komi Oil Spill Reported

LD1612160994 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1543
GMT 16 Dec 94

[By BUSINESS-TASS correspondent Aleksander
Samozhnev]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Moscow December 16 TASS—
The volume of oil spilled as a result of the damage to the
oil pipeline in Usinsk proved to be five times larger than
the officially stated data and approaches 79 thousand
tonnes. However, the spill is not hazardous for the
environment outside Russia. This conclusion has been
drawn by a United Nations group of experts who have
been invited to assess the accident. At the same time, in
expert opinion, there is the need for a number of
protective measures and for decreasing rivers’ pollution.
This will make it possible to avoid a sharp deterioration
of the state of the environment and prevent the spill of
oil into the Barents Sea.

The United Nations group of experts arrived at the
conclusion that oil pollution is not the result of a
one-time spill, but of the accumulation of oil spilt over
many years, particularly in the summer and autumn of
1994, said Michael Elmqvist, chief of the group of
experts. It was established that a total area of 65 hectares
was polluted with oil.

Efforts of the United Nations department for humani-
tarian affairs and of experts of the United Nations
environment programme were first rallied in Komi,
Elmqvist said. Another United Nations mission will
arrive in Russia for inspection in April 1995. The next
stage will be to invite independent experts to estimate
the material damage from the leakage.

Ukrainian Prime Minister Vitaliy Masol paid an official

Masol had talks with Belarusian Prime Minister Chyhir.

relations and certain international problems of mutual
interest took place. Masol met Ukrainian businessmen
and Ukrainian diaspora representatives in the Republic
of Belarus.

A number of agreements were signed during the visit. It
was noted with satisfaction that fruitful cooperation
between the two countries was being established in
recent years in the political, trade, economic, scientific,
technical, cultural, law enforcement, and other sectors.
Readiness for further extension and consolidation of
bilateral cooperation in these directions was expressed.
The sides agreed to carry out coordinated actions in
resolving joint ecological problems. They were unani-
mous in assessing the scale of the disaster at the Cher-
nobyl nuclear power station. In connection with this the
necessity of close coordination between Belarus and
Ukraine in eliminating the disaster’s aftermath and more
joint involvement of the world community in resolving
this problem was confirmed.

Issues of Pelarusian-Ukrainian trade, economic, scien-
tific and technical cooperation occupied a central place

ination of topical international problems at the talks

Ukraine)—The activities of the CSCE mission in the

solving all disputes, Crimean President Yuriy Meshkov
said after the meeting witii CSCE representatives.

The CSCE delegation stayed in the Crimea for two days.
The visit, according to the CSCE mission leader Sla-
vomir Dabrova, was aimed at solving the problem about
the premises for the local representation.

Members of the CSCE mission met with leadership of
the Crimean parliament as well as with representatives
of the parliament factions.

Meeting with deputies of the Kurultai faction, CSCE
representatives were interested in the relationships
between the Crimean Tatars and all power branches in
the Crimea and Ukraine, the principle of the Crimean
Tatars settlement and the possibility of the threat on the
part of the Islamic fundamentalism.

According to the lester of the Kurultai faction, Refat
Chubarov, the CSCE mission in Ukraine and the Crimea
will help closer integration of Ukraine into Europe and
will help the European community understand better the
problems of the Crimean Tatars.

According to the commission’s mandate, one of the three
CSCE representatives in the Crimea will tackle the
problem of the Crimean Tatars.

Slavomir Dabrova said that the CSCE Commissar for
National Minorities Max van der Stoel plans his visit to
the Crimea on December 20.

private sector in that country. Its functioning will create

National Affairs
Chechnya Events Said To Influence Crimean
Situation

AUI612124694 Kiev MOLOD UKRAYINY in
Ukrainian 15 Dec 94 p I

[Report by Volodymyr Prytula, MOLOD UKRAYINY
special correspondent, from Simferopol: “Chechnya Is
Not Far Away From the Crimea”)

[FBIS Translated Text] “The invasion of Russian troops
and the beginning of war in Chechnya has given rise toa
deep concern among the Crimean democratic public.
Chechnya is much closer to the Crimean Peninsula than
geographic maps might suggest,” said in a conversation
with me Refat Chubarov, deputy of the Supreme Council
of the Crimean Autonomous Republic. Indeed, it is not
only the same religion, but also similar fates that unite
many Crimean citizens with residents of Chechnya. In
1944, both the Crimean Tatars and the Chechens were,
by the Moscow leadership’s order, deprived of their
homeland and forcibly deported into exile. It is, there-
fore, clear why there were many Crimean Tatars among
the people who gathered at a rally on 12 December in the
central square of Simferopol to voice their protest
against the Russian troops invading the territory of
Chechnya. However, as the speakers emphasized, repre-
sentatives of different nationalities and activists of all
Crimean democratic organizations gathered at the
meeting of solidarity with the Chechen people. In his
speech, Refat Chubarov pointed out that when the
Soviet troops invaded Czechoslovakia just a few persons

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

50 UKRAINE

in the USSR publicly protested, whereas now, hundreds
and thousands of Crimeans expressed their protest
jointly with the democratic public of Russia, Ukraine,
and other states. The participants in the Simferopol rally
called upon the Russian leadership “to immediately stop
all military actions against the Chechen people and to
withdraw Russian Armed Forces from the territory of
Chechnya.” In the resolution that was adopted, the
participants in the rally supported “the struggle of the
Chechen people in defense of its fatherland.”

R. Chubarov, chairman of the “Kurultay” parliamentary
faction, said in an interview for MOLOD UKRAYINY
that the events in Chechnya will have a serious effect
upon the situation around the Crimea. Having opted for
the armed intervention in Chechnya, Moscow has
deprived itself of a possibility to react if Kiev takes
drastic measures to bring the Crimean Constitution in
line with the Constitution of Ukraine.

The leaders of the Crimean parliament refuse to make
comments on the events in the Chechen Republic. Local
observers believe that, no matter how the situation
develops in the Caucasus, official Simferopol will end up
a loser. The Ukrainian leadership is getting a counter-
argument in its controversy with the Crimean separat-
ists.

LD1712230294 Moscow ITAR-TASS World Se: vice in

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Lev Ryabchikov]

[FBIS Translated Text] Simferopol, 17 Dec—Infantry

Guard and Army corps will now be on duty to guard
public law and order along with the militia. It is also
intended to introduce guard dog patrols in a few days.

Although all of this is reminiscent of “a procedure”’ for
introducing a state of emergency in the Crimean capital,
there are grounds for such actions. It was noted at an
emergency meeting of the leaders of the power structures
and executive committees today that a wave of crimes
has swept Crimea and automatic weapons and grenades
are being used more and more frequently. Anatoliy
Franchuk, head of the Crimean government, stressed
that “the crime situation today is such that one may
deviate from the established norms in some things.”

Cooperation with the military in combating crime will
continue all the time now, Vitaliy Kirichenko, acting
head of the internal affairs directorate of Crimea, said at
a news conference that took place immediately after the
meeting.

Replying to journalists’ questions, he and Ivan Kolomyt-
sev, head of the security service, did not confirm that

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

tens of Crimean Tatars, who had sworn to lay down their
lives—which was reported by some mass media—had
been sent to Chechnya. “We are taking measures to
prevent mercenaries being hired for Chechnya,” Ivan
Kolomytsev said. “And at the moment we do not have
any information about them being recruited.”

He also said that the guarding of several facilities had
been stepped up, airports in particular, in connection
with information received about the possible infiltration
into the territory of Ukraine by terrorist groups from the
Caucasus to carry out terrorist acts.

Parliamentary Groups Comment on Draft State
Power Law

LD1612133594 Kiev UNIAR in Ukrainian 2230 GMT
15 Dec 94

[FBIS Translated Text] Kiev, 15 Dec—The parliamen-
tary group of “Rukh” and groupings of “Reforms,”
“Statehood,” Interregional Bioc of Reforms and
“Center” have drawn up a joint document containing
comments on the draft constitutional law on state power
and local self-government, which is to be tinalized by the
body formed by the parliament and president. These
groups of deputies favor the presidential and parliamen-
tary form of government, proposing that the draft law’s
Article 17 should be formulated as follows: The Supreme
Council is authorized to address and decide all issues in
the life of the state and society that are in need of
legislative regulation.

The ;right-of-center parliamentary groups want the
Supreme Council of Ukraine to be able to dissolve itself
if it is unable to produce a majority fit to legislate.

The presidential draft provides for the division of central
executive power into three structures, namely the presi-
dential administration, Cabinet of Ministers, and other
ministries and departments.

‘Reforms’ Group Suggests Law Be ‘Reworked’

LD1712130294 Kiev Radio Ukraine World Service in

at a news conference on 16 December expressed their
view on the presidential draft of the law on state power

Viewing the submission of this document for the
Supreme Council’s consideration as the first step toward
establishing a democratic and constitutional system in
our state, the Reforms group at the same time has a
whole number of essential comments to be made on the
draft law. It was said, in particular, that the group’s

UKRAINE 51

a threat of the emergence of authoritarianism in the state
structure and failing to lay the foundation for the effec-
tive organization of structures and powers and for the
distribution and responsibilities of the branches and
levels of state power.

be substantially reworked by a joint group of presidential
administration and Supreme Council of Ukraine repre-
sentatives, which should include members of deputies’
groups and groupings.

se Council Elections Postponed for One
ear

LD1912102094 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1004
GMT 19 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondents Viktor Demidenko and
Mikhail Melnik]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Kiev December 19 TASS—
By-elections to the Ukrainian parliament in 46 constit-
uences are put off by one year. This decision was taken
by the Ukrainian parliament, since there were no win-
ners there even after ten rounds of voting.

The decision circulated by the legislature on Monday [19
December] says that elections in many constituences
were found invalid due to a low turnout.

Therefore, taking into account considerable financial
and material outlays from the budget, it was decided not
to hold elections for the tim. being. Only 404 legislators
will now work in the Ukrainian parliament.

Consumer Price Index Shows Ist Decrease in 4
Months

WS1912102794 Kiev INTELNEWS in English 0036
GMT 19 Dec 94

{Report by INTELNEWS staff writer Ed Zwirn: ““Con-
sumer Prices Decline After Four-Month Rise”’}

{[FBIS Transcribed Text] Consumer prices actually
declined over the past two weeks, causing the first
decrease in the INTELNEWS Consumer Price Index in
over four months.

Dollar and karbovanets holders fared equally well this
time around, with the cost of living declining by about 11!
percent for each.

Consumers, however, remain substantially worse off
than they were when the last decrease occurred 18 weeks
ago on Aug. 11. Since that time, prices have increased by
about 258 percent in the local currency and 7.4 percent
when reckoned in dollars.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Although prices had been increasing moderately before
that, a Cabinet of Ministers pricing decree issued Oct. 21
unleashed the spectacular round of price increases that
have caused profound repercussions throughout the
country. The decree, which raised prices on a host of
consumer staples and services such as bread, metro
tickets and rent, caused prices to jump immediately and
items to vanish from shelves as consumers rushed out at
the end of October to beat the price increases that they
correctly assumed would come at the beginning of
November.

In the six weeks that foliowed the issuance of decree,
prices shot up by 138 percent.

Now that all the price increases planned for the imme-
diate future have already taken effect and the supply
situation seems to be gradually adjusting itself, this trend
has moderated. In the prior two-week period ending Dec.
2, the increase had already started slowing down by
registering the shortest two-week gain (1.6 percent) since
September.

It of course remains to be seen whether this price
stabilization—which coincides with several weeks of
relative stability for Ukraine’s beleaguered currency—
represents a true respite or just a statistically insignifi-
cant pause from the ravaging inflation that seemed
capable of causing both political and labor unrest just a
short time ago.

For the most recent two-week period, the index dropped
by 11.5 percent, or about 299 percent annualized.

The overall increase since measurements began Feb. 24
is 246.6 percent, or about 199 percent annualized. The
index now measures 346.6, with Feb. 24 equal to 100.

The good news for consumers this time around came in
the food category as the reappearance of milk and cheese
in some state stores lessened their consolidated prices.
Although there were no actual price decreases for these
items, the availability of state milk and cheese caused
decreases of 51.9 percent and 26 percent, respectively.
The other component of the decrease was an 11.1
percent drop in the price of gasoline.

The bad news inciuded a 20 percent increase for pork, 15
percent for eggs and 13.1 percent for butter. The price of
a Snickers Bar also went up 13.3 percent.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Chyhir, Masol Express ‘Contentment’ With Visit

LD1612203094 Minsk Radio Minsk Network in
Belarusian 1700 GMT 16 Dec 94

[Report by correspondent Svyatlana Ilyichenka on news
conference of Belarusian Prime Minister Mikhail Chyhir
and Ukrainian Prime Minister Vitaliy Masol at the
National Press Center in Minsk on 16 December; incor-
porating recorded coniments by Chyhir and Masol]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Ukrainian Prime Minister
Vitaliy Andriyovych Masol, who was heading the gov-
ernment delegation, and Belarusian Prime Minister
Mikhail Mikhalayevich Chyhir expressed content with
the results of the visit as they said a single viewpoint had
been reached on a majority of the issues. Moreover, the
countries are close in spirit and culture and should have
normal goodneighborly and friendly relations. As a
result, the trade turnover between our countries will
double next year. Belarus has goods that interest Ukrai-
nian citizens and Belarus needs cooking oil, wheat, and
sugar that can be purchased from neighbors. Here is
what Mikhail Mikhalayevich Chyhir noted in particular:

[Chyhir] Another characteristic feature and point is that
our states are very close neighbors. Proceeding even
from world prices on transportation, which we now have
in the CIS, deliveries of goods from Ukraine are much
more advantageous than what can be brought from
Russia, from Siberia for example. And it would be
unforgivable not to use this advantage. Therefore, one
can sum it up in the following way: What has been lost
cannot be returned but what can be used in the interests
of our friendly neighboring nations should be done
without delay. And it should be done in a way to
minimize lost possibilities.

[Ilyichenka] Vitaliy Andriyovych Masol has invited
Mikhail Mikalayevich Chyhir to pay a return visit. Here
is a passage from the speech of the Ukrainian Prime
Minister.

{Masol] I must say that I am very pleased with the results
of the visit. There were several meetings with Mikhail
Mikalayevich. We also met the president of Belarus
twice today. Our conversations confirmed that in spirit,
strategy, and tactics our views coincide. Really, the time
of euphoria has run out; the time of the so-called—I
would put it in inverted commas—independence. We
are all dependent on one another. If we receive water
from Belarus along the Dnieper River we cannot block it.
And we will never say that we can live without the
Belarusian Dnieper. The agreements that we managed to
sign today give us a basis for further expansion of our
cooperation in order to simplify all our procedures. The
last agreement signed by us—I was already in my coat,
Mikhail Mikalayevich in a suit—was about our customs
services and border services. | would like to say we
should be closer to each other and spend less time

WESTERN REGION 53

dealing with formalities. We had many meetings today
and yesterday. We visited enterprises and met the lead-
ership of the republic. I must say that even yesterday's
meeting at the refrigerator factory gave me a lot person-
ally. A process of certain stabilization has started today.
The task of our governments is to sustain it at today’s
level. If we solve this task, I think that already in one to
two years stabilization in the economy will begin.
[Words indistinct] to repeat one phrase that today the
economy determines the politics and our people have
already understood that.

[llyichenka] Journalists asked a lot of questions this

[Chyhir] As far as the customs union is concerned, you
know that CIS countries are very close to an agreement
on a single customs space. As it happens, Ukraine is

Belarus are the closest to an agreement on a single
customs space. Therefore, our position is whatever turn
events take—I think that regardless of what happens
with other countries—in relations between Russia and
Belarus, the agreement on a single customs space will be
signed soon. All problems in relations with Russian
customs [words indistinct]. And I would put in the

on many positions, and we will carry on doing it all the

Officials Uncover Counterfeit Dollar Operation

LD1612153394 Moscow INTERFAX in English 1353
GMT 16 Dec 94

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Minsk, Dec 16 (Interfax-West)
- Belarusian Interior Ministry officers have discovered
an underground printing shop in Minsk engaged in
manufacturing counterfeit $100 notes, ministry press
center officials told Interfax.

The press was owned by an ethnic Armenian from Sochi,
who put up $11,000, and a researcher, from the General
and Inorganic Chemistry Institute under the Belarusian
Academy of Sciences, who had been working on pro-
tecting cash and securities from forgery.

The two men constructed a special press in the casting
equipment factory in Volkovysk, Grodno Region, and
purchased aluminum-copper plates in the mint.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

They admitted planning to print about $10 million for
subsequent sale in former Soviet republics at a wholesale
price of 50 cents a dollar.

Nearly 3000 counterfeit notes were seized.

the Moldovan schools’ teaching staff and parents to
teach in the Latin script. The 14th Army assisted
Tiraspol school No. 20, which was devastated. Van der
Stoel expressed his satisfaction with the dialogue he had
with General Lebed. According to some unverified infor-
mation, Max Van der Stoel was not satisfied with the
meeting with Igor Smirnov on grounds that the Tiraspol
authorities do not let Moldovans decide what language
to study.

Arguments Over Russian Peacemakers’ Future
Summed Up

MM 1612131594 Moscow ROSSIYSKAYA GAZETA in
Russian 15 Dec 94 First Edition p 7

[Report by Lyudmila Feliksova: ““What Color of Helmet
Will Go to Moldova? Moldovan Leadership Would Like
to See a More International Peacemaking Mission in Its
Republic]

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

[FBIS Translated Text] A Moldovan delegation went to
the Budapest meeting of leaders of the CSCE countries.
According to official information, Moldovan President
M. Snegur delineated the questions that interest the
republic. They are well known: On the one hand, the
final withdrawal of the Russian 14th Army; on the other
hand, the introduction of an international peacemaking
force into the Dniester Region security zone.

According to M. Snegur, international supervision of the
process of the withdrawal of the Russian army is in the
interests not only of Russia and Moldova, but of all those
who are “anxious to strengthen security and stability in
the European continent.” The Moldovan president
assigns the leading observer's role to the CSCE mission
stationed in the republic. The president sees the involve-
ment of multilateral forces in the peacemaking contin-
gent, of which Russian subunits have constituted the
nucleus hitherto, as the way to finally secure a political
settlement. M. Snegur stressed once again that the con-
flict was imposed from without (the sole formula used of
late by the Moldovan president to describe the nature of
the civil war in the Dniester Region) and added that he
was “prepared immediately and without hesitation to
join those who advocate introducing an international
peacemaking force.”

But the Dniester Region does not share this approach, to
put it mildly. The official Dniester Moldovan Republic

position in this connection was set out by Sh. Kitsak,
military adviser to the president of the unrecognized

WESTERN REGION 55

Dniester Republic. According to him, first of all the
status of the security zone has to be deteremined.
Second, the Dniester Region is opposed to a reduction of
Russia’s peacemaking forces and does not intend to let
anyone else into its ierritory.

“Observers from the United Nations and CSCE, by all
means,” Sh. Kitsak said, “but we will only recognize a
Russian peacemaking force.”

As for the “forces from without” that apparently
unleashed the conflict, Sh. Kitsak believes that this is a
blatant allusion to Russia, but decribes it as a “complete
distortion of the facts.”

So Moldova is saying thanks to the Russian peacemakers
who have been maintaining stability in the region for two
years, but it prefers to see others in the role of partners at
the stage of a future settlement.

The story goes that former Moldovan Security Minister
Plugaru, who was removed from the post together with
Defense Minister Kostash following the bloody events in
Bendery, liked to show journalists on the map the place
where there might be a NATO military base. At the time
this was regarded as some kind of joke. Later, having
now left his post, Plugaru said in an interview that
Turkey might take the Russian 14th Army’s place. This
also sounded pretty wild. Now the question who
Moldova would like to see replace the Russian influence
in the region appears to be taking a practical turn.

is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

Nazarbayev Receives European Community
Mission to Almaty

LD1612212494 Almaty Kazakhstan Television Network
in Russian 1500 GMT 16 Dec 94

{[FBIS Translated Text] President of Kazakhstan Nur-
sultan Nazarbayev today received Ambassador Robert
(Cramer), head of the European Community delegation,
on the occasion of his official accreditation in Kaza-
khstan.

The protocol on the establishment of diplomatic rela-
tions between the Republic of Kazakhstan and the
European Union [EU] was signed in 1992. One of the
main bodies of the EU is the European Community
Commission, whose mission in Kazakhstan is to be
headed by Mr. Cramer.

The European Community Commission is the executive
body of the European Union, possessing considerable
independence in the implementation of its functions.
The main trend of its activities is to assist the economic
and humanitarian development of the countries of the
former USSR.

Auezkhan Kurbasov, Kazakhstan’s extraordinary and
plenipotentiary ambassador to Belgium, was also
appointed representative of Kazakhstan in the European
Community Commission in 1993.

Mr. Cramer gave assurances that he would exert every
effort to step up the assistance by EU member countries
in the development of Kazakhstan.

Nursultan Nazarbayev expressed gratitude to the Euro-
pean Community for the opportunity to have its official
mission in Kazakhstan, and readiness to cooperate with
it in all areas. Our country already has good business
contacts with Germany and Austria, and intends to
strengthen relations with Italy, Spain, and Luxembourg.
With the help of the mission of the European Commu-
nity Commission, I think we will be able to systematize
and enlarge these relations, the president said, and
wished Mr. Cramer fruitful work.

Deputy Defense Minister on Russia, Army

LD1612164794 Almaty PANORAMA in Russian 10 Dec
94 pll

[Unattributed article: “Kazakhstan Probably Will
Abandon Universal Military Service in the Future”’]

{[FBIS Translated Text] Alibek Kasymov, Kazakhstan’s
first deputy defense minister and chief of general staff,
answers questions from PANORAMA.

[PANORAMA] Alibek Khamidovich, Russia and Kaza-
khstan signed an agreement on military coperation in
March. What is being done in this area today?

[Kasymov] This basis agreement makes concrete the
military and political aspects of the cooperation between
the two states. It was signed as a development of the
treaty on friendship and cooperation between our coun-
tries signed on 25 May 1992.

At the moment a further 24 documents, which elaborate
on the basis agreement, are being prepared. They apply
to the agreement’s practical implementation. The
majority of them have been already drawn up and
initialed. We hope that we will sign the main group of
these documents during the next meeting of heads of
state in Almaty on 23 December. Then 1995 will not be
a year of talks but of real practical actions, which will
strengthen the common security of our states.

[PANORAMA] What spheres do these documents touch
upon?

[Kasymov] Very different. Personnel training, military
service, the status of Russian military formations on
Kazakh territory, the technical provision of airbases and
communications. There are a number of documents of a
specific, purely professional nature.

An agreement on cooperation in the process of cutting
strategic nuclear arms on Kazakh soil and an agreement
on the procedure for conducting inspections in accor-
dance with START I are being prepared. All of the treaty
participants think that it will come into effect in the first
quarter of 1995. And this means that there will be a large
volume of practical work to do on strategic nuclear arms
reduction.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

58 CENTRAL ASIA

Unfortunately, so far we have not been able to achieve
an agreement on a major problem—the functioning of
testing sites on Kazakh territory.

[PANORAMA] Has the issue been resolved in principle?
Will Russia lease them or not?

[Kasymov] The Russian position on this issue should be
based on its national security interests.

[PANORAMA] There are several testing sites on Kazakh
territory—Sary-Shagan, Emba... What other ones? Is it
planned to sign separate agreements on each of them?

[Kasymov] We intend to decide on four sites. You have
named two of them. So there is also the Kapustin Yar
site, the military fields of which are in western Kaza-
khstan. And the air testing center in Aktyubinsk region.
A separate agreement will be reached for each of them
because they are all are different in their territorial
sweep, material reserves, and other features.

[PANORAMA] What are the sides’ main disagreements?

[Kasymov] Property and compensation problems. They
are quite complicated to resolve. These issues go beyond
the competence of our ministries. They have to be
resolved at government level. For the time being we
cannot make a decision on the sites’ residual value, a
method for determining the price of the lease, or an
approach to drawing up the lease contract.

We proposed in principle to consider these property and
compensation issues in the aggregate—in the course of
all the interests that Russia has on Kazakh territory. I
hope that our Russian counterparts will regard this
proposal with understanding.

At the same time it is proposed to reduce the area of the
testing sites. We understand that today’s economic situ-
ation in Russia is quite hard. Therefore we are not
setting very strict conditions but are trying to find
mutually acceptable solutions, which will take into
account the interest of both sides in the sphere of joint
defense.

[PANORAMA] For how long can can these sites be
leased?

[Kasymov] As long as we are allies. The term of the
military cooperation treaty has been defined. In prin-
ciple the same scheme can be applied for leasing the
sites.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

[PANORAMA] Alibek Khamidovich, you said “as as
long as we are allies.” How long then?

{[Kasymov] We are so much connected to Russia eco-
nomically and ethnically that in principle we see this
prospect as long-term. | think we will be with Russia as
long as Kazakhstan exists as a state itself.

[PANORAMA] Another point is that Russia itself has
not finally defined its position toward the near abroad
yet. Everything will take shape proceeding from how
soon and in what direction this happens.

[PANORAMA] Can Russian military bases be stationed
in Kazakhstan? Some time ago Russian Defense Min-
ister Pavel Grachev announced the intention of creating
them in CIS countries.

[Kasymov] This practice is well accepted in the rest of
the world. The allied states who are members of NATO,
ASEAN, and other blocs, if necessary, station their bases
on the territories of other states. A legal basis is created
for their operation. Military bases are not a topic of
discussions in relations with Russia on this matter. We
came to the agreement that there will be Russian Feder-
ation military units temporarily stationed on the terri-
tory of Kazakhstan. The principle of ex-territorialism is
absolutely not acceptable here. Currently, as I have said,
we are preparing an agreement about the status of such
units. We need to settle this from the point of jurisdic-
tion and determine their necessity and tasks to find out
to what degree they will guarantee the security of each
state. And if this meets the norms of international law
and the interests of the state we should accept it.

But there is an obstacle. According to its legislation, as of
1 January 1995 Russia cannot send its servicemen
beyond the boundaries of the state. The legal basis for
their presence in other states, including Kazakhstan, is
expiring. So this problem needs to be speedily resolved
in principle and then all the other issues on the structure,
purpose, and functioning of military units.

[PANORAMA] The press has already reported that
Kazakhstan has begun the withdrawal of strategic forces.
How is this being implemented?

{[Kasymov] Kazakhstan has displayed good will and,
even before START I comes into force, has conducted a
number of measures that have resulted in a considerable
reduction in strategic groups of Russian forces on
Kazakh territory.

Currently the withdrawal of intercontinental ballistic
missiles from combat duty is being carried out, and
components of strategic offensive weapons are being
withdrawn. The aviation unit stationed near Semipal-
atinsk has been completely withdrawn. Special products
are being transferred to Russia for storage, utilization,
and further processing.

The process is going on in quite an organized way,
excluding interruptions in the financial provision for

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

these measures. The treaty stipulates the completion of
these works within seven years.

[PANORAMA] How many strategic weapons are sta-
tioned in Kazakhstan?

[Kasymov] This data is available from public sources.
But I would rather not to hurry to repeat them.
According to the treaty, the participating countries of
START I have to exchange ratification documents on
5-6 December in Budapest during the CSCE summit. In
this connection an exchange of more precise data will
take place in accordance with established procedure.

[PANORAMA] How do you see the future, the functions
and tasks of Kazakhstan’s army, which is currently being
formed?

[Kasymov] Taking into account my experience of mili-
tary service in the Soviet Army, I want it to be a
qualitatively new army, small in size, completely
equipped, well trained, and professional. The recruit-
ment principle should be revised. We might decide to
abolish universal military service, where people are
obliged to serve, which results in the creation of an
unattractive image for the army.

The current situation demands a flexible and sensible
approach to defining the structure and number of the
forces, and the orientation of military training so as not
to undermine the economy of the state. I would call this
period transitional.

In essence, not only Kazakhstan’s armed forces but the
forces of ali other former USSR states are functioning in
a post-Soviet regime. And we need to act and reform
them not by the method of testing and mistakes but by
precise thought, calculation, and thoroughly checked
actions. The army is a special state institution and there
can be no haste in reforming it.

[PANORAMA] Is there a concept for transformations?
And what will be the size of the Kazakh Army?

[Kasymov] The concept is being worked out. You may
ask why the principle reforms have not been achieved
over two and a half years? Everybody, including me as
the chief of staff, needs time to realize what stage the
army is at, and clearly and precisely study the state of
affairs. Having determined this, the decision can be
taken about which path to take for further development
and implemenmt it in practice.

As far as size is concerned, it is well known that an army
should be one percent of the population. For Kazakhstan
this means 170,000. In fact we have even less. And it’s
future size depends on how strong the economy will be
and on the tasks linked with defense sufficiency.

Currently it is too early to speak about it. We are at the
beginning of our path. But I am sure we have made the
right choice. The transition period will last at least five
years. I think it will be completed in 2000-2005. And

CENTRAL ASIA 59

that is when it will be possible to judge the real results of
military reform in Kazakhstan.

Chechnya Viewed as Integral Part of Russia

LD1612204694 Moscow ITAR-TASS in English 1831
GMT 16 Dec 94

[By ITAR-TASS correspondent Vadim Lagutin]

[FBIS Transcribed Text] Rabat December 16 TASS—
Chechen developments provoke concern of states-
members of the Organization of the Islamic Conference
and the whole international community, Deputy Kazakh
Foreign Minister Vyacheslav Gizzatov told ITAR-TASS
here today.

He stressed that “Kazakhstan considers Chechnya to be
an integral part of the Russian Federation and thinks
that it is an internal Russian affair.” At the same time, he
said that the problem has a humanitarian aspect. A
military way to settle the problem always entails loss of
life. That is why, Kazakhstan wants Russia to settle the
problem with negotiations and political methods, added
Gizzatov.

Time has not been missed; maximum patience is needed
since this is the matter of the people of Russia, he said.
“Problem solution by force will bring political dividends
for none, if tanks and aircraft are used to put under fire
one’s own people,” said Gizzatov.

Kazakhstan hopes that the Russian and Chechen leader-
ship have enough wisdom and common sense to prevent
a hard trial of the state stability, said Gizzatov.

Gizzatov attended a session of the Organization of the
Islamic Conference at the level of foreign ministers and
a summit conference of the organization. In his words,
the stand of Kazakhstan is shared by the overwhelming
majority of the organization member-states.

Nazarbayev Addresses Conference on National
Issues

LD1712123494 Almaty KAZAKHSTANSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 14 Dec 94 pl

[FBIS Translated Excerpt] A republican confercace on
the socioeconomic and sociopolitical situation in the
country has been taking place in Almaty since 13
December. Those invited to the conference include
heads and deputy heads of departments on social issues
in regional administrations, presidential staff members,
officials of the Cabinet of Ministers, heads of a number
of ministries and departments, and senior editors of
republican and regional newspapers.

Deputy Prime Minister Akhmetzhan Yesimov and State
Counsellor Marat Tazhin delivered reports. It was noted
that the last three years of the transitional period, which
were difficult and very hard for the republic, allow us, on
the basis of our own experience, to understand the

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copy 1g and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

60 CENTRAL ASIA

essence of the ongoing processes and analyze their
dynamics and basic tendencies, and our own actions.

[passage omitted]

The central focus of all the main difficulties of the
government and the whole society concerns the financial
and account sphere and the sphere of money circulation.
Socioeconomic problems are directly reflected in the
budget, which itself is alarming. The annual collection of
taxes and payments will barely exceed 50 percent of what
was planned. The nonpayments crisis, which has been
brought about by indecision in introducing extremely
strict bankruptcy procedures, is also playing a negative
role.

This has resulted in difficulties in the payment of wages
and pensions and in funding social support measures for
needy people.

As of 1 November 1994 arrears of wages in budget-
funded establishments was 1.2 billion tenge throughout
the republic. Around 15 billion is owed to workers by
enterprises for which the state is not responsible.

A difficult situation is arising as regards the payment of
pensions, debts for which totaled 3,384 million tenge as
of 21 November. The government believes that giving
budget status to the pension fund was a big mistake. Now
it is has been corrected, but on the whole the situation
has not yet been restored.

By speaking frankly about extremely complicated prob-
lems like these, we should bring them to the notice of the
population and explain that the government is working
intensively on their solution.

Meanwhile, not everything in the wages and pensions
sphere looks gloomy. Thanks to current policy, the
inflation rate was kept to 0.6 percent a day in October
and November. For the last few months the salary
growth index has outstripped prices. According to pre-
liminary data, the average salary in the republic in
October was 3,090 tenge, and according to the center of
regional nutrition issues the cost of the consumer basket
was 1,352.2 tenge a month.

As for the republic’s sociopolitical situation, over the last
three years the press has gained freedom and a number of
new parties and sociopolitical movements have
appeared, and a good number of their members are
working in the professional parliament.

Kazakhstan’s independence is becoming stronger. Its
sovereignty is secured even more reliably after a number
of relevant documents were signed in Budapest. [Presi-
dent} Nursultan Nazarbayev’s initiative on creating a
Eurasian union has attracted the attention of all peoples
of the former USSR.

Analytical reports were also made by the heads of a
number of ministries and departments.

President Nursultan Nazarbayev addressed the confer-
ence. He spoke in detail about topical problems of the
republic’s domestic and foreign policy.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

In particular, the head of state said that since the collapse
of the USSR Kazakhstan has in fact been left alone with
its own problems caused by the one-sided orientation of
industry toward raw materials, and a hypertrophied
agriculture in pursuit of target figures. As the country
gained genuine sovereignty, we had to start many things
practically from zero. And the first thing was to carry out
structural reconstruction of the economy, introduce
market relations, and achieve the genuine democratiza-
tion of society in difficult conditions. Those who do not
see or understand this are bound to have preconceptions
or to have a blinkered view of the processes taking place
in real life.

But unless we abandon objectivity, we cannot help
noticing that strategically Kazakhstan has chosen the
right path of development. Its authority is growing in the
world community. Its ties with all countries of the far
and near abroad, and first and foremost with the Russian
Federation, are moving to civilized, equal, and mutually
beneficial relations. We regard our great northern neigh-
bor—the special relations of partnership and traditional
ties of friendship with which go back to times immemo-
rial—as our natural ally.

Nursultan Nazarbayev continued by saying that many of
our troubles are already behind us. For example, we are
clearly on course to overcome the economic crisis and
curb inflation. he position of the tenge is strengthening;
the size of wages, pensions, and social allowances has
started to grow, though not as rapidly as we would like.

Interethnic unity and accord should be preserved in
order to broaden and extend the long awaited changes.
The single people of the country should not be divided
on ethnic and social lines, but should solve our common
tasks together and in agreement. The position of every
Kazakhstan citizen is now assessed not from his words,
but from his practical contribution to the common
welfare, from his patriotism and constructivism, and
from his efforts to bring a better future nearer.

The head of state stressed that we are building an open
society. There should be pluralism of opinions, and
every Kazakh citizen should have the oppurtunity to
openly express his personal point of view. I am quite
convinced that there is no place in society for corpo-
ratism or intrigue for achieving exclusively personal or
group interests. People are already tired of all of this.
They understand better that genuine democracy and
market are completely opposed to the passions aroused
by mass meetings or policies of confrontation. The
people no longer want to support those who do not
concentrate on specific and fruitful work.

Deputy Prime Minister on Encouraging Signs in
Economy

LD1612121494 Almaty SOVETY KAZAKHSTANA in
Russian 13 Dec 94 p 1

[Unattributed article: “The Forecasts Raise Hopes”™]

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

[FBIS Translated Text] The republic’s sick economy is
showing the first symptoms of getting well. This conclu-
sion can be drawn from the results of the news confer-
ence held by Deputy Prime Minister Viktor Sobolev last
Friday [9 December].

First, the fact that we managed to stop the fall in
industrial production to some extent gives hope. Last
year, compared to 1992, there was a 24 percent drop; this
year, compared to 1993, there was a 14 percent drop.
And if we take the last three months, a slight increase can
be seen. A similar increase can be observed in retail
turnover.

Moreover, it is typical that having become joint-stock
companies, many industrial enterprises switch to market
relations and use nontraditional methods. The experi-
ence, for example, of the Merey furniture factory situ-
ated in the capital is significant: it managed to find a
foreign partner and convince it of the profitability of
joint production. Today, investments from the devel-
oped capitalist country help the furniture makers keep
afloat and conduct their business rather profitably and
sell their produce not only in Kazakhstan but in the near
abroad as well.

As before, the raw material branches of industry are
operating stably. The extraction of iron ore, coal, and gas
is being increased. There are customers for our copper.
Tractor production is increasing. However, the problem
of payments to neighboring countries for produce is still
acute. Let us say that the residents of the capital have
experienced all the “delights” of mutual payments for
Uzbek gas, finding themselves short of it during the
winter frosts.

But extraordinary approaches have appeared in the issue
of payments. The practice, for example, of the Russian
Tveruniversnab and Ekibastuz Ugolbank deserves only
approval; they pay for each other’s produce with prom-
issory notes. And although the form of the payments has
not been worked out on the scale of the two countries
and the nominal cost of the bills has not yet been
determined, everything has been decided at the level of
the two commercial structures.

The dynamics of our wages growth also gives grounds for
optimism. Strange though it may seem, it has out-
stripped inflation on average as well. In November, the
general level of inflation was 114.2 percent while the rate
of wage growth was 126.1 percent. It is also interesting
that our wages lead the wages race in the CIS. Since the
beginning of this year to November they increased in
Kyrgyzstan by 1.85 times, in Russia by 2.2, in Uzbeki-
stan by 5, and in Ukraine by 7; in Kazakhstan they
increased by 9.4 times.

Here the reader has the right to smile ironically because
we all know too well that Russian wages are higher than
Kazakh wages. And this is true. As is the fact that
Turkmen wages outstripped both. Here is some objective
statistical data: With his month’s salary a resident of

CENTRAL ASIA 61

Turkmenistan can buy eight consumer baskets of the
eight most necessary foodstuffs, a Russian can buy 4.26
such baskets, and a resident of Kazakhstan only 3.59. It
is true that comparison with other republics of the CIS is
in our favor. For example, an Uzbek and a Moldovan
can buy only 1.8 such baskets but a Kyrgyz even less—
1.6. That is, we are ahead of them here.

Despite the gloomy forecasts of the skeptics, our national
monetary unit is gradually strengthening its position as
well. At any rate, the republic has not experienced
anything like “Black Tuesday” yet, which hit the Russian
ruble hard. Moreover, the tenge is holding steady at
50-55 to the dollar while the Russian currency is falling
several points daily.

And though our national currency is being pushed into a
secondary role by dollars and marks and it often has to
play ihe role of Cinderella, this will change radically if
the Cabinet of Ministers accepts the proposal of the
National Bank to make our monetary unit the sole
means of payment in the republic from | January 1995,
as has already been reported earlier.

Answering my question about to whether the govern-
ment will support this, the deputy prime minister said
unambiguously: “The Cabinet of Ministers itself initi-
ated this step. It is wrong for certain people to make
fortunes on the differences in rates. The tenge will
become the complete master of the ball in the interests of
the state and the majority of us, starting from the New
Year.”

As you can see, the signs of stabilization of Kazakhstan’s
economy are present and the government's forecasts
made before the New Year give cause for hope—as long
as nothing unpredicted happens. As the saying goes, God
forbid it should go wrong...

Kyrgyzstan

Uzbeks Play Political Games With Gas Supply

LD1712143094 Bishkek VECHERNIY BISHKEK in
Russian 17 Dec 94 pl

{Unattributed article: “Major Games Using Gas”]

{FBIS Translated Text] The gas crisis Kyrgyzstan is
experiencing is far from being an accident. And it has
been caused not only by economic reasons. As in past
years Tashkent is beginning to turn off the taps at the
beginning of winter. It is interesting that these games
began in the Soviet period. There were periods when
Leonid Brezhnev several times arbitrated between Kyr-
gyzstan and Uzbekistan. Moscow pressured Kyrgyzstan
if we turned off the water from the Toktogul reservoir,
and Uzbekistan if the supply of fuel was lessened.

Kyrgyzstan today is the most exemplary payer in the
CIS. Bishkek has paid Tashkent 80 percent of the cost of
fuel used. Refrigerated trucks with selected meat and
trains loaded with glass and other goods are despatched

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

62 CENTRAL ASIA

to Uzbekistan every day. Moreover the representatives
of the government of the neighboring republic are free to
pick and choose. And making use of this state of affairs,
they are over-picky in their approach to every type of
goods proposed by Kyrgyzstan.

For our part the water supply to Ferghana has not veen
stopped either last year or this summer. If such a thing
were to happen, Kyrgyzstan could have turned the most
densely populated regions of Uzbekistan to salt, and
Tashkent could have lost thousands of tonnes of agricul-
tural production sent from Ferghana for export. But....

We will quote some figures for the sake of clarity. Now
our republic owes 12 million doliars to Uzbekistan for
gas, Georgia owes 350 million dollars, Armenia owes 200
million dollars to Turkmenistan, Belarus owes up to
three billion dollars to Russia, and Ukraine owes about 1
billion dollars just to Turkmenistan. Turkmenistan has
not yet stopped fuel supplies to any countries yet.

The same Uzbekistan has never turned off the pipelines
to the European part of the CIS, despite a major accident
in Karakalpakstan last week. There was a major gas leak
in the pipeline supplying the European part of the CIS.
And at the same time energy supplies to Kazakhstan and
Kyrgyzstan are permanently reduced every winter.

The reason is the political games Tashkent plays. Islam
Karimov does not tire of reminding his neighbors who is
the guarantor of stability and the first claimant to the
leading role in Central Asia.

How many years and winters wiil this game continue?
Obviously, until Kyrgyzstan becomes completely self-
sufficient in electricity. This will be much cheaper and
more reliable. For now, if inhabitants were to pay the full
cost of gas, then they would have to pay an average of
500 soms per month. For the time being the gas supply
issue has been resolved until the end of the year, as the
government has reported. Soon the Kyrgyz delegation
will leave for Tashkent to agree on gas supplies for next
year.

a Signs Edict on Local Heads of Bishkek,

LD1612172894 Bishkek Kyrgyz Radio First Program
Network in Russian 1400 GMT 16 Dec 94

[Decree “On the Heads of Local Administration in
Bishkek and Osh” signed by President Askar Akayev;
place and date not given}

[FBIS Translated Text] With a view to further devel-
oping local self-government and improving relations
with the state authority in the cities that are the largest
local associations in the country and play a special role in
its public, political, and cultural life—Bishkek, the cap-
ital of the Kyrgyz Republic, and Osh, the political and
historical center in the south of Kyrgyzstan—I decree:

1. The heads of local self-government in Bishkek and
Osh are the highest officials in local self-government and
the representatives of the president of the Kyrgyz
Republic in these cities. They are accountable for con-
ducting state policy and implementing executive power
there.

2. The heads of local self-government in Bishkek and
Osh shall be elected directly by the people of these cities
for a term of five years.

3. The post of the head of the local state administration
is abolished in Bishkek and Osh.

4. Candidates for head of local self-government in
Bishkek and Osh are put up for election by the President
of the Kyrgyz Republic on an alternative basis, following
consultations with Bishkek and Osh city councils.

5. Elections of the heads of local self-government in
Bishkek and Osh are to be held on 5 February 1995.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

6. The first deputy head and deputy heads of local
self-government in Bishkek and Osh shall be appointed
by the heads of local self-government of these cities in
agreement with the relevant councils.

7. The status of the heads of local self government of
Bishkek and Osh is regulated by the law of the Kyrgyz
Republic on local self-government and local state aamin-
istration in the Kyrgyz Republic.

8. The organization and conduct of elections of the heads
of local self-government in Bishkek and Osh is placed on
the Central Electoral Commission for holding referen-
dums and elections in the Kyrgyz republic.

9. This decree comes into force upon publication.

[signed] Askar Akayev, president of the Kyrgyz
Republic.

Text of Tajik Constitution

LDI1512114094 Khudzhand LENINABADSKAYA
PRAVDA in Russian 30 Nov 94 pl

[Text of the Tajik Constitution adopted on 6 November
1994] .

{[FBIS Translated Text] [Preamble] We, the people of
Tajikistan, as an inseparable part of the world commu-
nity, seeing ourselves responsible and duty bound to
past, present, and future generations, wishing to ensure
the sovereignty, development, and perfection of our
state; recognizing the rights and freedoms of the indi-
vidual as sacred; affirming the equality of rights and
friendship of all nationalities and peoples of Tajikistan;
seeking to built a just society; adopt and declare as valid
this constitution.

Chapter One. Fundamentals of the Constitutional
Structure

Article 1. The Republic of Tajikistan is a sovereign,
democratic, law-governed, secular, and unitary state.

Tajikistan is a social state, its policy is aimed at pro-
viding relevant living conditions for everybody.

The names Republic of Tajikistan and Tajikistan are of
equal validity.

Article 2. The state language of Tajikistan is Tajik.
Russian is a language of communication between the
nationalities.

All nationalities and peoples living on the territory of the
republic are entitled freely to use their mother tongue.

Article 3. The state symbols of the Republic of Tajikistan
are the flag, emblem, and national anthem.

Article 4. The capital of Tajikistan is the city of Dus-
hanbe.

Article 5. The life, honor, dignity, and other rights of the
individual are sacred.

Recognition, observance, and protection of human and
civil rights and freedoms is the obligation of the state.

Article 6. In Tajikistan the people are the expression of
sovereignty and the sole source of power of the state, and
they exercise them directly or through their representa-
tives.

The citizens of Tajikistan, despite their nationalities,
make up the people of Tajikistan.

No social association, group of people, or individual has
the right to seize state power. The seizure of power is the
gravest crime.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

64 CENTRAL ASIA

Only the president and the Supreme Assembly [Majlis-e
Oli] has the right to speak on behalf of all the people of
Tajikistan.
Article 7. The territory of Tajikistan is indivisible and
inviolable.

Tajikistan consists of Gornyy Badakhshan Autonomous
Oblast, oblasts, towns, rayons, settlements, and villages.

The state ensures the sovereignty, independence, and
territorial integrity of the republic. Agitation and actions
aimed at disunity of the state are prohibited.

The constitutional law defines the procedure of estab-
lishing and changing of administrative and border units.

Article 8. In Tajikistan public life is to develop on the
basis of political and ideological diversity.

No ideology, including religious ideology, is granted the
status of state ideology.

Social associations are established and will function
within the framework of the constitution and laws. The
state provides equal opportunities for their activities.

Religious organizations are separate from the state and
cannot interfere with state affairs.

The establishment and /; nctioning of social associations
that encourage racism, nationalism, social and religious
enmity, and hatred, as well as advocate the forcible
overthrow of the constitutional structures and the for-
mation of armed groups is prohibited.

Article 9. State power is exercised on the basis of the
separation of legislative, executive, and judicial powers.

Article 10. The Constitution of Tajikistan has supreme
legal authority and its norms have direct application.
Laws and other legal acts that run counter to the consti-
tution are of no legal validity.

The state and all its bodies, officials, citizens, and their
associations are duty bound to observe and implement
the constitution and laws of the republic.

International legal documents recognized by Tajikistan
are a constituent part of the legal system of the republic.
If republican laws do not conform to the recognized
international legal documents, the norms of the interna-
tional documents apply.

International laws and documents recognized by Tajiki-
stan apply following official publication.

Article 11. Tajikistan will implement a peaceful policy,
respecting the sovereignty and independence of other
states of the world and will determine foreign relations
on the basis of international norms.

Agitation of war is prohibited.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Proceeding from the supreme interests of the people,
Tajikistan can join or withdraw from international asso-
ciations and organizations and establish relations with
foreign countries.

The state will cooperate with compatriots living abroad.

Article 12. The economy of Tajikistan is based on
various forms of ownership.

The state will guarantee freedom of economic activity,
entrepreneurship, equality of rights, and the protection
of all forms of ownership including private ownership.

Article 13. Land, bowels of the earth, water, airspace,
animal and vegetable kingdoms, and other natural
resources are owned by the state, and the state guaran-
tees their effective use in the interests of the people.

Chapter Two. Basic Duties of Individuals and Citizens

Article 14. The freedoms and rights of individuals and
citizens are protected by the constitution, the laws of the
republic, and international documents recognized by
Tajikistan.

In implementing rights and freedoms, limitations in the
constitution and laws are allowed only to ensure the
rights and freedoms of others, public order, and to
safeguard the constitutional structure and the territorial
integrity of the republic.

Article 15. A person will be regarded as a citizen of
Tajikistan if he is a citizen of the Republic of Tajikistan
on the day of adoption of the constitution.

Any connection between the citizensh‘p of Tajikistan
and the citizenship of another state is not recognized,
except in cases indicated by the law and interstate
treaties of Tajikistan.

The procedure for acquiring and forfeiting citizenship is
defined by law.

Article 16. A citizen of Tajikistan outside the country
will be protected by the state. No citizen of the republic
will be extradited to a foreign state. Extradition of a
criminal to a foreign state will be resolved on the basis of
mutual agreement.

Foreign citizens and stateless persons enjoy the rights
and freedoms and have the responsibilities and duties of
citizens of Tajikistan except in cases stipulated by law.

Tajikistan will offer political asylum to foreign citizens
whose human rights are violated.

Article 17. All are equal before the law and the courts.
The state guarantees the rights and freedoms of every
person regardless of nationality, race, sex, language,
religious beliefs, political persuasion, social status,
knowledge, and property.

Men and women have the same rights.

Article 18. Every person has the right to life.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

No one can be deprived of life except by order of the
court for the gravest crime.

The state guarantees the inviolability of a person. No one
will be subjected to torture, punishment, and inhuman
treatment. It is prohibited to subject a person to forced
medical or scientific experiments.

Article 19. Every person is guaranteed judicial protec-
tion. Every person is entitled to demand that his case be
considered by a competent and impartial court.

No one can be arrested, kept in custody, and exiled
without a legal basis. Every person is entitled to legal
assistance from the moment of his arrest.

Article 20. No one is adjudged guilty of a crime except by
the sentence of a court in accordance with the law.

No one carries responsibility if the term of investigation
expires or his committed action is not regarded as a
crime. No one can be tried twice for a crime.

A law that is adopted after the commission of an illegal
act by a person and that envisages severe punishment for
that act shall not be retroactive. If, after the commission
of an illegal act a punishment is not envisaged or a light
punishment is envisaged, the new law is applicable.

Total confiscation of the property of a convicted person
is prohibited.

Article 21. The law shall safeguard the rights of the
victim.

The state guarantees judicial protection and compensa-
tion to the victim.

Article 22. The home of a person is inviolable.

It is prohibited to enter the home of a person by force
and deprive a person of a home except in cases pre-
scribed by law.

Article 23. Privacy of correspondence, telephone conver-
sations, and the postal and communication rights of each
person are ensured, except in cases prescribed by law.

The collection, storage, utilization, and dissemination of
information about a person’s private life without his
consent is not permitted.

Article 24. Every citizen has the right freely to choose
their place of residence, to leave the republic, and to
return to it.

Article 25. State bodies, social associations, and officials
are obliged to ensure that every person has the opportu-
nity to seek and see documents affecting their rights and
interests except in cases prescribed the by law.

Article 26. Every person has the right freely to determine
their position toward religion, to profess any religion
individually or together with others or not to profess any,
and to take part in religious customs and ceremonies.

CENTRAL ASIA 65

Article 27. Every citizen has the right to take part in
political life and state administration directly or via their
representatives.

Citizens have equal rights to state service.

Every citizen has the right to elect and be elected from
the age of 18.

Citizens deemed incompetent by a court or who have
been deprived of liberty in accordance with a court
sentence do not have the right to take part in the
elections and referendums.

Elections and referendums are held on the basis of
universal, equal, and direct suffrage in a secret ballot.

Article 28. Every person has the right to be united. A
person has the right to join in voluntarily and leave
political parties, trade unions, and other social associa-
tions.

Article 29. Citizens have the right to take part in meet-
ings, rallies, demonstrations, and peaceful processions
prescribed by law. No one can be forced to take part in
the aforementioned activities.

Article 30. Every person is guaranteed freedom of
speech, publishing, and the right to use means of mass
information.

State censorship and prosecution for criticism is prohib-
ited.

The list of information constituting a state secret is
specified by law.

Article 31. A citizen has the right to apply personally or
together with a group of people to the authorities.

Article 32. Every person has the right to ownership and
inheritance.

No one is permitted to suspend and limit the individ-
ual’s right to ownership. The property of an individual is
taken away only on the basis of the '«, with the consent
of the owner and to meet the requirements of the state
and society, and with the state paying full compensation.

Any material and spiritual damage inflicted on an indi-
vidual as a result of illegal actions by state bodies, social
associations, or individuals will be compensated in
accordance with the law.

Article 33. The state protects the family as the basis of
society.

Every person has the right to form a family. Men and
women who have reached the age of marriage have the
right freely to marry without any hindrance. In marriage
and in divorce, husband and wife have equal rights.
Polygamy is prohibited.

Article 34. A mother and child are entitled to special care
and protection by the state.

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

66 CENTRAL ASIA

Parents are responsible for the upbringing of children,
and adult children of working age are responsible for
care and provision of parents.

The state cares for the protection, upbringing, and edu-
cation of orphaned children.

Article 35. Every person has the right to work, to choose
their profession or job, and to have work protection.
Wages for work cannot be less than the minimum wage.

Any limitation is prohibited in labor relations. Equal
wages shall be paid for the same work.

Forced labor is not permitted, except in cases defined by
law.

Using women and child labor is prohibited in heavy and
underground works and in harmful conditions.

Article 36. Every person has the right to housing. This
right is ensured by means of construction of state, public,
cooperative and private housing.

Article 37. Every person has the right to leisure. This
right is ensured by establishing working weeks, hours
and days, paid annual leaves, weekly days off, and other
conditions prescribed by law.

Article 38. Every person has the right to health care. This
right is ensured by providing free medical assistance in
state medical establishments and by measures aimed at
protecting environment, developing mass sport, physical
training, and tourism.

Other types of medical assistance one can receive are
defined by law.

Article 39. Every person is guaranteed social security in
old age, in the event of sickness and disability, loss of
ability to work, or loss of a guardian or other instances
prescribed by law.

Article 40. Every person has the right freely to take part
in the cultural life of society; artistic, scientific, and
technical creation; and to use their achievements.

The state protects cultural and spiritual riches.
Intellectual property is protected by law.

Article 41. Every person has the right to education. The
basic general education is compulsory. The state guaran-
tees access to free general, vocational, and according to
abilities based on competition, general specialized and
higher education in the state educational establishments.

Other forms of acquiring education are defined by law.

Article 42. On the territory of Tajikistan every person is
obliged to observe the constitution and the laws and to
recognize the rights, freedoms, dignity, and honor of
others.

Ignorance of the law is no defense.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

Article 43. The protection of the homeland; safeguarding
the interests of the state; and strengthening the indepen-
dence, security, and defense capabilities of the homeland
are the sacred duties of citizens.

The procedure for military service is specified by law.

Article 44. The protection of natural, historical, and
cultural heritage are the duties of every person.

Article 45. Every person is obliged to pay taxes and
duties specified by law.

Laws introducing new taxes or making the economic
situation of the taxpayer difficult cannot retroactive.

Article 46. A state of emergency is declared as a tempo-
rary measure to ensure the citizens’ and state’s security
in instances of direct threat to the rights and freedom of
citizens, the state’s independence, its territorial integrity,
and natural disasters, that result in the republic’s consti-
tutional authorities being unable to act normally.

The period of a state of emergency is up to three months.
The president of Tajikistan can prolong this period in
necessary circumstances.

Article 47. During a state of emergency the rights and
freedoms stipulated in Articles 16, 17, 18, 19, 20, 22, 25,
and 28 of the constitution are not limited.

The Supreme Assembly is not dispersed during the
period of a state of emergency.

The law determines the legal regime of a state of emer-
gency.
Chapter Three. The Supreme Assembly [Majlis-e Oli]

Article 48. The Supreme Assembly is the highest repre-
sentative and legislative body of the Republic of Tajiki-
stan.

The Supreme Assembly is elected for a five-year term.

A citizen can be elected to the Supreme Assembly when
he reaches the age of 25.

The establishment and activity of the Supreme Assembly
are specified by law.

Article 49. The powers of the Supreme Assembly:

1. The enactment and amending of laws and orders and
their annulment;

2. Interpretation of the constitution and laws;

3. Determination of the basic directions of the republic’s
domestic and foreign policy;

4. Establishment of the Central Electoral and Refer-
endum Commission;

5. Specifying the date for a referendum;

6. Suggesting draft laws and other important issues of the
state and society for a nationwide referendum;

This report may contain copyrighted material. Copying and dissemination
is prohibited without permission of the copyright owners.

FBIS-SOV-94-243
19 December 1994

7. Specifying the date for election of the president;

8. Specifying the date for election of higher and local
representative bodies;

9. Approving the establishment and abolition of minis-
tries and state committees;

10. Ratification of the decrees of the president on
appointment and dismissal of the prime minister and
other members of the government;

11. Ratification of the decrees of the president on
appointment and dismissal of the chairman of the
National Bank and his deputies;

12. Election and removal of the chairman, deputy
chairmen, and judges of the Constitutional Court; the
Supreme Court; and the Supreme Economic Court at the
proposal of the president of the republic;

13. Approving the election and removal of the procu-
rator general and his Geputies;

14. Ratification of socioeconomic programs and
approval of economic assistance to other states;

15. Ratification of the state budget; determination of the
possible amount of the state budget deficit and the
sources of compensation for it; supervision over execu-
tion of the budget, determination of taxation and loan
policy;

16. Specifying the monetary system;

17. Specifying the structure, establishing, abolishing, and
changing administrative territorial units in necessary
circumstances;

18. Ratification and annulment of international treaties;
19. Establishment of courts;

20. Ratification of the decrees of the president on a state
of war and a state of emergency;

21. Establishing state awards;

22. Establishing military and diplomatic ranks and other

23. Approval of state symbols;
24. Declaration of general amnesty;

25. Annulment of the decrees of the presidium of the
Supreme Assembly contradicting the constitution and
other laws;

26. Implementation of uther duties prescribed by the
constitution and laws.

Article 50. The basic form of the activity of the Supreme
Assembly is the session.

A session of the Supreme Assembly is convened by the
presidium of the Supreme Assembly not less twice a year.

CENTRAL ASIA 67

A session of the Supreme Assembly convenes only when
attended by at least two-thirds of the people’s deputies.

The first session of the Supreme Assembly is convened
by the Central Electoral and Referendum Commission
one month after the election of people’s deputies.

Article 51. For the purpose of organizing the work of the
Supreme Assembly and the implementation of the
powers stipulated by the constitution and the laws, the
Presidium of the Supreme Assembly under the leader-
ship of the Supreme Assembly chairman will be estab-
lished.

The composition of the Presidium of the Supreme
Assembly includes the Supreme Assembly chairman,
first deputy chairman, deputy chairmen, and chairmen
of the committees and standing commissions of the
Su >reme Assembly.

The Supreme Assembly can elect other people’s deputies
as members of the Presidium of the Supreme Assembly.

Article 52. The Presidium of the Supreme Assembly has
authority:

1. To convene of the Supreme Assembly;
2. To prepare for sessions of the Supreme Assembly;

3. To coordinate the work of the committees and
standing commissions of the Supreme Assembly;

4. To organize interparliamentary relations;

5. To oversee the activities of the publications of the
Supreme Assembly and to appoint and dismiss editors-
in-chief of the publications;

6. To approve the appointment or recall of the heads of
diplomatic missions of Tajikistan in foreign countries
and republican missions at international organizations;

7. To implement other powers stipulated in the consti-
tution an¢ other laws.

The Presidium of the Supreme Assembly adopts deci-
sions within the framework of its authority.

Article 53. The Supreme Assembly elects from among
the people’s deputies the chairman of the Supreme
Assembly, first deputy chairman, deputy chairmen, and
chairmen of the committees and standing commissions
of the Supreme Assembly. One of the deputy chairmen of
the Supreme Assembly will be a people’s deputy repre-
senting Gornyy Badakhshan Autonomous Oblast.

The chairman of the Supreme Assembly will be elected
by a secret ballot by a majority of the total number of
people’s deputies.

Article 54. The chairman of the Supreme Assembly has
authority:

1. To exercise leadership and direction over issues that
will be discussed at the sessions;
